the fourth step is justified by
(ii)
,
and the fifth step uses
\det M\neq 0
along with (
4.1
).
∎

4.2.
Determinant problem for nonzero
field elements

As an application of the previous lemma, we now prove that the characteristic
matrix of the determinant problem
\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}
for
any two nonzero field elements
a,b
has small spectral norm.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field
with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements. For each
u\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},

define
G_{u}
to be the matrix with rows and columns indexed by
elements of
\mathbb{F}^{n\times n},
and entries given by

(4.3)

(G_{u})_{X,Y}=\begin{cases}q^{-n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{-1}&\text{if }\det(X+Y)=u,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then

(4.4)

\displaystyle\|G_{u}\|_{1}=1,

\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\|G_{v}-G_{u}\|\leqslant\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{-3/2}\leqslant 8q^{-3(n^{2}-1)/2},

\displaystyle u,v\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\}.

Proof.

Equation (
4.4
) follows from (
4.2
).
For (
4.5
), there are two cases to consider.
If
u=v,
then
G_{v}-G_{u}=0
and thus
\|G_{v}-G_{u}\|=0.
If

u\neq v,
write
G_{v}-G_{u}=[q^{-n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\lvert^{-1}g_{u,v}(X+Y)]_{X,Y}

with
g_{u,v}
as defined in Lemma
4.1
. Then

(4.6)

\|G_{v}-G_{u}\|=\frac{\|\widehat{g_{u,v}}\|_{\infty}}{\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}\leqslant\frac{1}{\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{3/2}},

where the first step applies Fact
2.10
,
and the second step uses Lemma
4.1
(iii)
.
It remains to simplify the bound of (
4.6
):

\frac{1}{\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{3/2}}=\left(\frac{|\mathcal{M}_{n}|}{q-1}\right)^{-3/2}=\left(q^{n-1}\prod_{i=0}^{n-2}(q^{n}-q^{i})\right)^{-3/2}\leqslant 8q^{-3(n^{2}-1)/2},

where the first step uses (
4.1
), the second step
applies Proposition
2.17
, and the last step is justified
by Proposition
2.13
.
∎

Lemma
4.2
was originally obtained by Sun
and Wang
[
27
]
using a different
and rather technical proof. By contrast, the proof presented above
is short and uses only basic Fourier analysis. With this newly obtained
bound on the spectral norm of the characteristic matrix of
\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}

for nonzero
a,b
, we can use the approximate trace norm method to
obtain a tight communication lower bound for this special case of
the determinant problem.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field,
and
n
a positive integer. Then for every pair of distinct elements

a,b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\}
and every
\gamma\in(0,1),

(4.7)

Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}(\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n})\geqslant\frac{1}{4}(n^{2}-3)\log|\mathbb{F}|-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{12}{\gamma}.

Proof.

Let
F
be the characteristic matrix of
\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}
.
For
u\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},
define
G_{u}
as in Lemma
4.2
.
Since
G_{a}
and
G_{b}
are supported on disjoint sets of entries,
(
4.4
) leads to

(4.8)

\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|_{1}=\|G_{b}\|_{1}+\|G_{a}\|_{1}=2.

Taking
\Phi=G_{b}-G_{a}
in Proposition
2.9
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\|F\|_{\Sigma,1-\gamma}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|}\left(\sum_{\dom F}F_{A,B}(G_{b}-G_{a})_{A,B}-(1-\gamma)\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|_{1}-\sum_{\overline{\dom F}}|(G_{b}-G_{a})_{A,B}|\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|}\left(\sum_{\dom F}|(G_{b}-G_{a})_{A,B}|-(1-\gamma)\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|_{1}}{\|G_{b}-G_{a}\|}

(4.9)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\,\gamma|\mathbb{F}|^{3(n^{2}-1)/2},

where the second and third steps are valid because
G_{b}-G_{a}

by definition coincides in sign with
F
on
\dom F
and vanishes
on
\overline{\dom F}
; and the last step uses (
4.5
)
and (
4.8
). Now (
4.7
) follows
from (
4.9
) in view of Theorem
2.23
.
∎

We remind the reader that Theorem
4.3
was obtained
with different techniques by Sun and Wang
[
27
]
,
who settled the determinant problem
\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}
for
nonzero
a,b
and left open the complementary case when one of
a,b

is zero.

4.3.
Determinant problem for arbitrary
field elements

Recall that the
rank versus determinant problem,

\RANKDET_{k,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}
,
is a hybrid problem that naturally generalizes the matrix rank problem

\operatorname{RANK}_{k,n}^{\mathbb{F},n,n}
and the determinant problem
\DET_{0,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}
.
Specifically, the rank versus determinant problem requires Alice and
Bob to distinguish matrix pairs with
\rk(A+B)=k
from those with

\det(A+B)=a,
where
a
is a nonzero field element,
k
is an
integer with
k<n,
and
A,B
are Alice and Bob’s respective inputs.
We will now construct a dual matrix for
\RANKDET_{k,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}

and thereby obtain a lower bound on its approximate trace norm. As
a dual matrix, we will use a linear combination of the dual matrices
from our analyses of the rank and determinant problems.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
n>k\geqslant 1
be given integers.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements,
and let
a\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\}.
Let
F
be the characteristic
matrix of
\RANKDET_{k,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}.
Then for all reals
\delta\geqslant 0

and all nonnegative integers
\ell,m
with
\ell+m\leqslant k,

(4.10)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{150}\left(1-\delta-\frac{64}{q^{m+1}}\right)q^{\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}\,q^{n^{2}},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1-\delta}{150}\cdot q^{k/2}\,q^{n^{2}}.

Proof.

This proof combines our ideas in Theorems
3.14

and
4.3
, and our dual matrix here will be
a linear combination of the dual matrices used in those theorems.

Fix nonnegative integers
\ell,m
with
\ell+m\leqslant k,
and let
\varphi\colon\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\to\mathbb{R}

be the corresponding function constructed in Lemma
3.9
.
This univariate function gives rise to a matrix
E_{\varphi},
described
in Definition
3.10
. To restate equation (
3.21
)
from our proof of Theorem
3.14
,

(4.12)

\|E_{\varphi}\|\leqslant 128\|\varphi\|_{1}\,q^{-\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}\,q^{-n^{2}}.

For
u\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},
define
G_{u}
as in Lemma
4.2
.
As our dual matrix, we will use

(4.13)

\Phi=E_{\varphi}+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{\varphi(n)}{q-1}\,(G_{a}-G_{b}).

Claim 4.5
.

For every matrix pair
(A,B),

\Phi_{A,B}=\begin{cases}(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}&\text{if }\det(A+B)=0,\\
\varphi(n)q^{-n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{-1}&\text{if }\det(A+B)=a,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

If
\det(A+B)=0,
then by definition
(G_{u})_{A,B}=0
for every
nonzero field element
u.
As a result, (
4.13
)
gives
\Phi_{A,B}=(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}
in this case.

In what follows, we treat the complementary case when
\det(A+B)\neq 0.

For all such matrix pairs,

(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\varphi(i)(E_{i})_{A,B}=\varphi(n)(E_{n})_{A,B}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}\lvert\mathcal{M}_{n}\lvert}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}(q-1)\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert},

where the first three steps are immediate from Definition
3.10
,
and the last step uses (
4.1
). In particular,

(4.14)

\Phi_{A,B}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}(q-1)\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{\varphi(n)}{q-1}\,((G_{a})_{A,B}-(G_{b})_{A,B}).

If
\det(A+B)=a,
then by definition
(G_{a})_{A,B}=q^{-n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert^{-1}

and
(G_{b})_{A,B}=0
for all
b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\},

so that (
4.14
) gives

\Phi_{A,B}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}(q-1)\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{\varphi(n)}{(q-1)q^{n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}.

If, on the other hand,
\det(A+B)=c
for some
c\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\},

then by definition
(G_{a})_{A,B}=0
and likewise
(G_{b})_{A,B}=0

for every
b\neq c,
so that (
4.14
) simplifies to

\Phi_{A,B}=\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}(q-1)\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}-\frac{\varphi(n)}{(q-1)}(G_{c})_{A,B}=0.\qed

We proceed to establish key analytic and metric properties of
\Phi.

To begin with,

\displaystyle\|\Phi\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|E_{\varphi}\|+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{|\varphi(n)|}{q-1}\,\|G_{a}-G_{b}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|E_{\varphi}\|+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{\|\varphi\|_{1}}{q-1}\,\|G_{a}-G_{b}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 128\|\varphi\|_{1}\,q^{-\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}\,q^{-n^{2}}+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0,a\}}\frac{\|\varphi\|_{1}}{q-1}\cdot 8q^{-3(n^{2}-1)/2}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leqslant(128q^{-\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}+8q^{-(n^{2}-3)/2})q^{-n^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{1},

where the first step uses the triangle inequality, and the third step
is a substitution from (
4.12
) and equation (
4.5
)
of Lemma
4.2
. To simplify this bound,
recall from the theorem hypothesis that
n>k\geqslant 1
and
\ell,m\geqslant 0.

Therefore,
\ell(k-\ell-m+1)\leqslant\ell(k-\ell+1)\leqslant(k+1)^{2}/4\leqslant n^{2}/4\leqslant n^{2}-3.

This results in
q^{-(n^{2}-3)/2}\leqslant q^{-\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}
, and
thus (
4.15
) simplifies to

(4.16)

\|\Phi\|\leqslant 136q^{-\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}q^{-n^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{1}.

Next, we examine
\|\Phi\|_{1}.
We have

\sum_{\rk(A+B)=n}|\Phi_{A,B}|=\sum_{\det(A+B)=a}|\Phi_{A,B}|=\sum_{\det(A+B)=a}\frac{|\varphi(n)|}{q^{n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}=|\varphi(n)|,

where the first and second steps are immediate from Claim
4.5
,
and the last step applies (
4.2
). Also,

\sum_{\rk(A+B)<n}|\Phi_{A,B}|=\sum_{\rk(A+B)<n}|(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}|=\|E_{\varphi}\|-\sum_{\rk(A+B)=n}|(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}|=\|\varphi\|_{1}-|\varphi(n)|,

where the first step uses Claim
4.5
,
and the last step invokes Lemma
3.11
. These two
equations yield

(4.17)

\|\Phi\|_{1}=\|\varphi\|_{1}.

Continuing,

\displaystyle\sum_{\dom F}F_{A,B}\Phi_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\det(A+B)=a}\Phi_{A,B}-\sum_{\rk(A+B)=k}\Phi_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\det(A+B)=a}\frac{\varphi(n)}{q^{n^{2}}\lvert\SL(\mathbb{F},n)\rvert}-\sum_{\rk(A+B)=k}(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\varphi(n)-\varphi(k)

\displaystyle=|\varphi(n)|+|\varphi(k)|

(4.18)

\displaystyle=\|\varphi\|_{1}-\sum_{r\notin\{k,n\}}|\varphi(r)|,

where the second step uses Claim
4.5
,
the third step invokes Lemma
3.11
and (
4.2
),
and the fourth step is valid due to Lemma
3.9
(i)
,
(ii)
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\sum_{\overline{\dom F}}|\Phi_{A,B}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rk(A+B)\notin\{n,k\}}|\Phi_{A,B}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rk(A+B)=n\\
\det(A+B)\neq a\end{subarray}}|\Phi_{A,B}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rk(A+B)\notin\{n,k\}}|\Phi_{A,B}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rk(A+B)\notin\{n,k\}}|(E_{\varphi})_{A,B}|

(4.19)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|,

where the second and third steps use Claim
4.5
,
and the last step uses Lemma
3.11
. Now

\displaystyle\sum_{\dom F}F_{A,B}

\displaystyle\Phi_{A,B}-\delta\|\Phi\|_{1}-\sum_{\overline{\dom F}}|\Phi_{A,B}|

\displaystyle=\|\varphi\|_{1}-\delta\|\varphi\|_{1}-2\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|

(4.20)

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(1-\delta-2\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|\right)\|\varphi\|_{1},

where the first step uses (
4.17
)–(
4.19
),
and the last step is legitimate by Lemma
3.9
(i)
.

Proposition
2.9
implies, in view of (
4.16
)
and (
4.20
), that

(4.21)

\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}\geqslant\frac{1}{136}\,\left(1-\delta-2\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|\right)q^{\ell(k-\ell-m+1)/2}\,q^{n^{2}}.

Since
\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|\leqslant 32q^{-m-1}
by Lemma
3.9
(v)
,
this proves (
4.10
). The alternative lower
bound (
4.11
) follows by taking
\ell=k

and
m=0
in (
4.21
) and noting that
\sum_{r\notin\{n,k\}}|\varphi(r)|=0

in this case (by Lemma
3.9
(iii)
).
∎

By virtue of the approximate trace norm method, Theorem
4.4

yields the following tight lower bound on the communication complexity
of the rank versus determinant problem.

Theorem

(restatement of Theorem
1.7
)
.

There is an absolute constant
c>0
such that for every finite field

\mathbb{F},
every field element
a\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},

and all integers
n>k\geqslant 0,

(4.22)

Q_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4|\mathbb{F}|^{k/3}}}^{*}(\RANKDET_{k,a}^{\mathbb{F},n})\geqslant c(1+k^{2}\log|\mathbb{F}|).

Proof.

For
k=0,
the claimed lower bound follows from the fact that
\RANKDET_{0,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}

is nonconstant and hence has communication complexity at least
1

bit. For
k\geqslant 1,
our lower bounds on the approximate trace norm
of
\RANKDET_{k,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}
are identical to those for
\operatorname{RANK}_{k,n}^{\mathbb{F},n,n}

(Theorems
4.4
and Theorem
3.14
,
respectively). Accordingly, the proof here is identical to that of
Theorem
3.15
, with equations (
4.10
)
and (
4.11
) of Theorem
4.4

used in place of the corresponding equations (
3.16
)
and (
3.17
) of Theorem
3.14
.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain an optimal communication lower bound for
the unrestricted determinant problem.

Theorem

(restatement of Theorem
1.6
)
.

There is an absolute constant
c>0
such that for every finite field

\mathbb{F},
every pair of distinct elements
a,b\in\mathbb{F},

and all integers
n\geqslant 2,

(4.23)

Q_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4|\mathbb{F}|^{(n-1)/3}}}^{*}(\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n})\geqslant cn^{2}\log|\mathbb{F}|.

Proof.

If
ab=0,
then
\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}
contains as a subproblem
either
\RANKDET_{n-1,b}^{\mathbb{F},n}
(when
a=0
) or
\neg\RANKDET_{n-1,a}^{\mathbb{F},n}

(when
b=0
), and therefore (
4.23
)
follows from Theorem
1.7
. If
a
and
b
are
both nonzero, Theorem
4.3
gives

Q_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4|\mathbb{F}|^{(n-1)/3}}}^{*}(\DET_{a,b}^{\mathbb{F},n})\geqslant c^{\prime}n^{2}\log|\mathbb{F}|-\frac{1}{2}\log 24

for a small enough constant
c^{\prime}>0.
Taking a weighted average of
this lower bound with the trivial lower bound of
1
bit settles (
4.23
).
∎

5.
The subspace sum and intersection
problems

As discussed in the introduction, our analysis of the subspace sum
and subspace intersection problems has similarities with the rank
problem but also diverges from it in important ways. Instead of additively
composed matrices whose rows and columns are indexed by elements of

\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n\times n}
, we now have matrices with rows and
columns indexed by subspaces, and each entry
(A,B)
depends solely
on the dimension of
A\cap B
. While the construction of the univariate
dual object is similar to that for the rank problem, its relation
to the singular values of the dual matrix is significantly more intricate,
and computing the spectral norm of the dual matrix is now a challenge.
Our study of the spectral norm is based on ideas due to Knuth
[
12
]
.
We start in Section
5.1

by formalizing the equivalence of the subspace sum and subspace intersection
problems, which allows us to focus on the latter problem from then
on. As a first step toward solving the subspace intersection problem,
we collect necessary technical results about subspace combinatorics
in Section
5.2
. In Section
5.3
,
we give a formal definition of subspace matrices, state several auxiliary
results, and compare our analysis of their spectrum to that of Knuth.
In Section
5.4
, we fully
determine the spectrum of subspace matrices. In Sections
5.5
–
5.7
,
we use this spectral study along with our techniques developed in
Section
3
to prove optimal lower bounds on
the communication complexity of the subspace intersection problem.
Sections
5.8

and
5.9
conclude
with matching communication upper bounds. As in previous sections,
we let
q
denote an arbitrary prime power and adopt
\mathbb{F}_{q}

throughout as the underlying field.

5.1.
Equivalence of
the subspace sum and intersection problems

The equivalence of the subspace sum and subspace intersection problems
from the standpoint of communication complexity is a straightforward
consequence of the identity (
2.1
),
valid for any linear subspaces
S
and
T
in a finite-dimensional
vector space. We formalize this equivalence below.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative
integers with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.
Then for all integers
d,D

with
d\neq D,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SUM}_{d,D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}=\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{m+\ell-d,m+\ell-D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SUM}_{d}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}=\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{m+\ell-d}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}.

Proof.

Let
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n}
be arbitrary subspaces of dimension

m
and
\ell,
respectively. Since
\dim(S+T)=m+\ell-\dim(S\cap T),

we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{SUM}_{d,D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}(S,T)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{m+\ell-d,m+\ell-D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}(S,T),

settling (
5.1
). Analogously,
for any subspaces
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n}
of dimension
m

and
\ell,
respectively, we have
\dim(S+T)\leqslant d
if and only
if
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant m+\ell-d,
which implies (
5.2
).
∎

We will now prove our main result on the subspace sum problem (stated
in the introduction as Theorems
1.8

and
1.9
) assuming our corresponding
result on subspace intersection (Theorem
1.10
).
In the rest of this work, we will focus on proving Theorem
1.10
.

Proof of Theorems
1.8

and
1.9
assuming Theorem
1.10
.

Recall that Theorem
1.8
is
a special case of Theorem
1.9
, corresponding
to
\gamma=1/3
. Therefore, it suffices to prove Theorem
1.9
.
Define
r=m+\ell-D
and
R=m+\ell-d.
Then the hypotheses
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant d<D\leqslant\min\{m+\ell,n\}

and
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(2d-m-\ell)/5},{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}]

of Theorem
1.9
can be equivalently
stated as

(5.3)

\displaystyle\max\{0,m+\ell-n\}\leqslant r<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\gamma\in[{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5},{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}].

Recall from Proposition
5.1
that
\operatorname{SUM}_{d,D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

is the same function as
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R,r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
,
which in turn is the negation of
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{r,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
.
Now the bounds for
\operatorname{SUM}_{d,D}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
claimed in
Theorem
1.9
follow from the bounds
for
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{r,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
in Theorem
1.10
,
upon substituting
R=m+\ell-d.
This appeal to Theorem
1.10

is legitimate due to (
5.3
) and (
5.4
).

Analogously,
\operatorname{SUM}_{d}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
is the same function
as
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
(Proposition
5.1
),
and therefore the bounds claimed for
\operatorname{SUM}_{d}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

in Theorem
1.9
follow from the bounds
for
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
in Theorem
1.10
,
upon substituting
R=m+\ell-d.

∎

5.2.
Counting subspaces satisfying
combinatorial constraints

When it comes to counting, one could hope that the transition from
subsets to subspaces would be straightforward and amount to replacing
binomial coefficients with their Gaussian counterparts. Unfortunately,
this is not the case. Many basic results for sets have no analogues
in the subspace setting. For example, the well-known inclusion-exclusion
formula (
2.1
) is valid for
two subspaces but does not generalize to any larger number. As a consequence,
it is in general a subtle task to count the subspaces of a given dimension
that satisfy basic combinatorial constraints relative to other given
subspaces. We start by counting, for given subspaces
A
and
C
,
all
d
-dimensional subspaces that contain
C
and avoid
A\setminus C
.

Lemma 5.2

(Counting subspaces externally)
.

Let
A
and
C
be linear subspaces
of an
n
-dimensional vector space
V
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let

d\geqslant 0
be an integer. Then the number of dimension-
d
linear
subspaces
X
such that
C\subseteq X\subseteq V
and
A\cap X=A\cap C

is

(5.5)

q^{(\dim(A)-\dim(A\cap C))(d-\dim(C))}\binom{n-\dim(A+C)}{d-\dim(C)}_{q}.

Proof.

The lemma is trivially true for
d\notin[\dim(C),n]
since the Gaussian
binomial coefficient in (
5.5
) is zero
in that case. In what follows, we consider the complementary case

d\in[\dim(C),n].

Let
\mathscr{X}
be the set of subspaces
X
in the statement of
the lemma. Fix a basis
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\dim(C)}
for
C.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be the set of all
d
-tuples
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\dim(C)},u_{1},\ldots,u_{d-\dim(C)})

of vectors in
V
such that for all
i,

(5.6)

u_{i}\notin A+C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{i-1}\}.

Then each element of
\mathscr{B}
is an ordered basis, with

(5.7)

|\mathscr{B}|=\prod_{i=1}^{d-\dim(C)}(q^{n}-q^{\dim(A+C)+i-1}).

Claim 5.3
.

Every subspace
X\in\mathscr{X}
has precisely

(5.8)

\prod_{i=1}^{d-\dim(C)}(q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)+i-1})

ordered bases in
\mathscr{B}
.

Proof.

Let us say that a sequence of vectors
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k})

in
X
is
good
if (
5.6
) holds
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k.
We will prove that for every good sequence
of
k
vectors in
X
, where
k<d-\dim(C),
there are exactly
q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)+k}

vectors
u_{k+1}\in X
such that the sequence
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k+1})

is good. Indeed, letting
S,S^{\prime},T
in Fact
2.3

be the subspaces
X,C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\},A,
respectively,
we obtain

\displaystyle X\cap(A+C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\})

\displaystyle=(X\cap A)+C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\}

\displaystyle=(C\cap A)+C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\}

\displaystyle=C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\}.

Therefore, the only vectors
u_{k+1}\in X
for which the sequence

(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k+1})
is not good are the elements of
C+\Span\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\},

which is a subspace of dimension
\dim(C)+k
because it is spanned
by the linearly independent vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\dim(C)},u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}.

In conclusion, out of the
q^{d}
vectors of
X
, there are precisely

q^{\dim(C)+k}
vectors
u_{k+1}
for which the sequence
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k+1}

is not good.

It now follows immediately that the number of good sequences
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{d-\dim(C)})

of vectors in
X
is (
5.8
) as claimed, with
q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)}

ways to choose
u_{1},
then
q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)+1}
ways to choose

u_{2}
given
u_{1},
then
q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)+2}
ways to choose

u_{3}
given
u_{1},u_{2},
and so on.
∎

Claim 5.4
.

Every element of
\mathscr{B}
is an
ordered basis for some subspace in
\mathscr{X}
.

Proof.

Fix a tuple
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{d-\dim(C)})
with (
5.6
)
for all
i,
and let

X=\Span\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\dim(C)},u_{1},\ldots,u_{d-\dim(C)}\}.

Then clearly
X
is a
d
-dimensional subspace with
C\subseteq X\subseteq V.

This in particular means that
A\cap X
contains
A\cap C.
It remains
to prove the opposite inclusion,
A\cap X\subseteq A\cap C.
For
this, fix arbitrary scalars
\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}
such that

\sum\alpha_{i}v_{i}+\sum\beta_{j}u_{j}\in A.

If some
\beta_{j}
were nonzero, we could take
j^{*}=\max\{j:\beta_{j}\neq 0\}

and obtain
u_{j^{*}}\in\beta_{j^{*}}^{-1}(A-\sum\alpha_{i}v_{i}-\sum_{j<j^{*}}\beta_{j}u_{j}),

contradicting (
5.6
). This means that
\beta_{j}=0

for all
j,
with the consequence that the vector
\sum\alpha_{i}v_{i}+\sum\beta_{j}u_{j}=\sum\alpha_{i}v_{i}

belongs to
C.
This settles the containment
A\cap X\subseteq A\cap C

and completes the proof.
∎

Claims
5.3
and
5.4
imply
that
|\mathscr{X}|
is the quotient of (
5.7
) by (
5.8
),
namely,

\displaystyle|\mathscr{X}|

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d-\dim(C)}\frac{q^{n}-q^{\dim(A+C)+i-1}}{q^{d}-q^{\dim(C)+i-1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{q^{\dim(A+C)}}{q^{\dim(C)}}\right)^{d-\dim(C)}\binom{n-\dim(A+C)}{d-\dim(C)}_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{(\dim(A)-\dim(A\cap C))(d-\dim(C))}\binom{n-\dim(A+C)}{d-\dim(C)}_{q}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 5.5

(Counting subspaces internally)
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be linear subspaces
in a vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
d\geqslant 0
be an integer.
Then the number of dimension-
d
linear subspaces
T
with
S^{\prime}\subseteq T\subseteq S

is

(5.9)

\binom{\dim(S)-\dim(S^{\prime})}{d-\dim(S^{\prime})}_{q}.

Proof.

Set
V=S,

C=S^{\prime},
and
A=\{0\}
in the statement of Lemma
5.2

∎

We now generalize Lemma
5.2
by allowing

A\cap X
to be any subspace of
A
of a given dimension
t
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
A,B
be linear subspaces
of an
n
-dimensional vector space
V
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Define

r=\dim(A\cap B).
Let
d
and
t
be nonnegative integers. Then
the number of dimension-
d
linear subspaces
X
such that
B\subseteq X\subseteq V

and
\dim(A\cap X)=t
is

(5.10)

q^{(\dim(A)-t)(d-t-\dim(B)+r)}\binom{n-\dim(A)-\dim(B)+r}{d-t-\dim(B)+r}_{q}\binom{\dim(A)-r}{t-r}_{q}.

Proof.

The lemma is trivially true for
t\notin[r,\dim(A)]
since the last
Gaussian binomial coefficient in (
5.10
)
is zero in that case. In what follows, we consider the complementary
case
t\in[r,\dim(A)]
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be the set of all dimension-
d
subspaces
X

with
B\subseteq X\subseteq V
and
\dim(A\cap X)=t.
Let
\mathscr{A}

be the set of all dimension-
t
subspaces
A^{\prime}
with
A\cap B\subseteq A^{\prime}\subseteq A.

By Corollary
5.5
,

(5.11)

|\mathscr{A}|=\binom{\dim(A)-r}{t-r}_{q}.

For any
X\in\mathscr{X},
the subspace
A\cap X
is by definition
a dimension-
t
subspace of
A
that contains
A\cap B
. This makes
it possible to define a function
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{A}

by
f(X)=A\cap X.

Claim 5.7
.

For every
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A},

(5.12)

|f^{-1}(A^{\prime})|=q^{(\dim(A)-t)(d-t-\dim(B)+r)}\binom{n-\dim(A)-\dim(B)+r}{d-t-\dim(B)+r}_{q}.

Proof.

Define
C=A^{\prime}+B.
Then
A\cap C=A^{\prime}+A\cap B
by Fact
2.3
,
which in view of
A\cap B\subseteq A^{\prime}
further yields

(5.13)

A\cap C=A^{\prime}.

Now

\displaystyle|f^{-1}(A^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|\{X:\text{$X$ is a subspace of dimension }d\text{ with }B\subseteq X\subseteq V\text{ and }A\cap X=A^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=|\{X:\text{$X$ is a subspace of dimension }d\text{ with }C\subseteq X\subseteq V\text{ and }A\cap X=A^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=|\{X:\text{$X$ is a subspace of dimension }d\text{ with }C\subseteq X\subseteq V\text{ and }A\cap X=A\cap C\}

(5.14)

\displaystyle=q^{(\dim(A)-\dim(A\cap C))(d-\dim(C))}\binom{n-\dim(A+C)}{d-\dim(C)}_{q},

where the first step is immediate from the definitions of
\mathscr{X}

and
f
; the second step holds because the condition
B\subseteq X

is logically equivalent to
A^{\prime}+B\subseteq X
due to
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
;
the third step applies (
5.13
); and the final step
uses Lemma
5.2
.

It remains to calculate the dimensions of the relevant subspaces in (
5.14
).
We have
\dim(C)=\dim(A^{\prime}+B)=\dim(A^{\prime})+\dim(B)-\dim(A^{\prime}\cap B),
which
along with
A^{\prime}\cap B=A\cap B
and
\dim(A^{\prime})=t
yields

(5.15)

\dim(C)=t+\dim(B)-r.

It is immediate from (
5.13
) that

(5.16)

\dim(A\cap C)=t.

Finally, we have
\dim(A+C)=\dim(A+A^{\prime}+B)=\dim(A+B)
and therefore

(5.17)

\dim(A+C)=\dim(A)+\dim(B)-r.

Substituting (
5.15
)–(
5.17
) into (
5.14
),
we arrive at the sought equality (
5.12
).
∎

Claim
5.7
implies that
|\mathscr{X}|

is the product of the right-hand side of (
5.11
)
and the right-hand side of (
5.12
), as was to
be shown.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be linear
subspaces in a vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
d
and
t

be nonnegative integers. Then the number of dimension-
d
linear
subspaces
T\subseteq S
with
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T)=t
is

(5.18)

q^{(\dim(S^{\prime})-t)(d-t)}\binom{\dim(S)-\dim(S^{\prime})}{d-t}_{q}\binom{\dim(S^{\prime})}{t}_{q}.

Proof.

Invoke Lemma
5.6
with
V=S,

A=S^{\prime},

and
B=\{0\}.

∎

5.3.
Subspace matrices

In
[
12
]
, Knuth defined
combinatorial matrices
of type
(n,t)
as matrices whose rows and columns are indexed by

t
-element subsets of a fixed
n
-element set, and whose
(A,B)

entry depends only on
|A\cap B|
. We begin with analogous definitions
in the setting of linear subspaces. Let
\mathbb{F}
be a given finite
field. For each
d=0,1,2,\ldots,n,
fix an ordering on the set of
dimension-
d
subspaces of
\mathbb{F}^{n}.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.

For any
r\geqslant 0,
define
J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
to be the
matrix whose rows are indexed by dimension-
m
subspaces of
\mathbb{F}^{n},

columns indexed by dimension-
\ell
subspaces of
\mathbb{F}^{n},

and entries given by

(J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})_{A,B}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\dim(A\cap B)=r,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where the row index
A
and column index
B
use the ordering on
the subspaces of
\mathbb{F}^{n}
fixed at the beginning.

Thus, the
(A,B)
entry of
J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
depends
only on the dimension of
A\cap B
rather than the subspaces
A,B

themselves. By passing to the linear span of all such matrices for
fixed
\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell
, we obtain a matrix family that we call

subspace matrices
.

Definition 5.10

(Subspace matrices)
.

For a function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},
we
define

(5.19)

J_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(r)J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}.

Recall that throughout this manuscript, the underlying field
is
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{q}
for an arbitrary prime power
q.

To avoid notational clutter, we will write simply
J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}

and
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
to mean
J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

and
J_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
, respectively.

To determine the eigenvalues of combinatorial matrices with rows and
columns indexed by
t
-element subsets of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, Knuth
investigates a certain homogeneous system of linear equations with
variables indexed by
s
-element subsets and the equations themselves
corresponding to
(s-1)
-element subsets. He refers to the solutions
to such systems as
(n,s)
-kernel systems
. It turns out that
the linear space of kernel systems has a basis supported on variables
labeled by a certain type of sets, which Knuth calls
basic sets

and which he fully describes in a combinatorial way. For any
s\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}

and any
(n,s)
-kernel system
(x_{u})
, he shows that the corresponding
vector
(z_{w})
, indexed by
t
-element subsets
w
and given
by
z_{w}=\sum_{u\subseteq w}x_{u}
, is an eigenvector for any combinatorial
matrix of type
(n,t)
. These vectors
(z_{w})
for various values
of
s
, together with the all-ones vector, make up a complete set
of eigenvectors, and Knuth’s analysis also reveals the associated
eigenvalues.

Even setting aside the more subtle combinatorial nature of subspaces
described in Section
5.2
,
it is not clear how to generalize Knuth’s notion of basic sets to
linear subspaces. For this reason, we do not appeal to combinatorial
machinery and rely instead on linear-algebraic arguments. As another
point of departure, our problem requires understanding the singular
values of a general subspace matrix
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell},
whereas
Knuth studied combinatorial matrices that are symmetric (analogous
to the symmetric subspace matrices
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
in our setting).
We note that the eigenvalues of symmetric subspace matrices
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}

were also determined by Delsarte
[
10
]
and Eisfeld
[
11
]
,
and their properties were studied in
[
3
,
8
]
.
However, these previous analyses do not seem to apply to the general
case of interest to us, namely, that of subspace matrices
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}

for arbitrary
m,\ell
.

We start by studying the subspace matrices
J_{k}^{n,m,k}
, which
play a particularly important role in our analysis. The following
lemma investigates their rank.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
n,m,k
be nonnegative integers with
m\geqslant k\geqslant 0

and
n\geqslant m+k
. Then

(5.20)

\rk J_{k}^{n,m,k}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}.

Proof.

In the degenerate case
n=0,
the matrix
J_{k}^{n,m,k}=J_{0}^{0,0,0}=\begin{bmatrix}1\end{bmatrix}

clearly has rank
\binom{0}{0}_{q}=1.
In what follows, we treat
the case
n\geqslant 1.
Here, we will exhibit reals
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}

such that for all
k
-dimensional subspaces
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
,

(5.21)

\sum_{X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}:\;\dim X=m}z_{\dim(A\cap X)}(J_{k}^{n,m,k})_{X,B}=\delta_{\dim(A\cap B),k}.

Put differently, this means that every vector of the standard basis

e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
can be obtained as a linear combination of the
rows of
J_{k}^{n,m,k},
immediately implying (
5.20
).
Rewriting (
5.21
),

(5.22)

\sum_{i=0}^{k}z_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}:\;\dim X=m,\\
\dim(A\cap X)=i\end{subarray}}(J_{k}^{n,m,k})_{X,B}=\delta_{\dim(A\cap B),k}\qquad\qquad\forall A,B.

The inner summation equals the number of
m
-dimensional subspaces

X
with
B\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
and
\dim(A\cap X)=i.

Applying Lemma
5.6
, we find that (
5.22
)
is equivalent to

(5.23)

\sum_{i=0}^{k}z_{i}q^{(k-i)(m-i-k+r)}\binom{n-2k+r}{m-i-k+r}_{q}\binom{k-r}{i-r}_{q}=\delta_{r,k},\qquad\qquad r=0,1,\ldots,k,

where
r
corresponds to
\dim(A\cap B)
in (
5.22
). Write (
5.23
)
in matrix form as

(5.24)

Mz=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{bmatrix}^{\sf T},

where
M=[M_{r,i}]
is the real matrix of order
k+1
given by

M_{r,i}=q^{(k-i)(m-i-k+r)}\binom{n-2k+r}{m-i-k+r}_{q}\binom{k-r}{i-r}_{q}

for
r,i\in\{0,1,\ldots,k\}.
All entries of
M
below the diagonal
are zero because
\binom{k-r}{i-r}_{q}=0
for
r>i.
The diagonal
entries, on the other hand, are

M_{r,r}=q^{(k-r)(m-k)}\binom{n-2k+r}{m-k}_{q},

which is nonzero because
n-2k+r\geqslant m-k
by the hypothesis that

n\geqslant m+k
. This makes
M
an upper triangular matrix with nonzero
entries on the diagonal. Then
M
is invertible, and a solution
z

to (
5.24
) is guaranteed to exist.
∎

We will recover the
k
-th eigenspace of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
as the
image of
\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}
under the linear map
J_{k}^{n,m,k}
.
The first step is to understand how the map
J_{i}^{n,m,k}
acts
on
\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}
for different values of
i
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
n\geqslant m\geqslant k
be positive integers.
Then for all
i=0,1,\ldots,k-1
and
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k},

(5.25)

J_{i}^{n,m,k}x=-q^{k-i-1}\cdot\frac{q^{i+1}-1}{q^{k-i}-1}J_{i+1}^{n,m,k}x.

Proof.

Consider the matrix product
M=J_{i}^{n,m,k-1}J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}
.
Let us compute the generic entry
M_{A,B},
where
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}

are subspaces of dimension
m
and
k
, respectively. By definition,

M_{A,B}
is the number of
(k-1)
-dimensional subspaces
X\subseteq B

such that
\dim(A\cap X)=i
. Invoking Corollary
5.8

with
S=B
and
S^{\prime}=A\cap B
, we obtain

(J_{i}^{n,m,k-1}J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k})_{A,B}=q^{(r-i)(k-1-i)}\binom{k-r}{k-1-i}_{q}\binom{r}{i}_{q},

where
r=\dim(A\cap B).
Rewriting this equation in matrix form,

J_{i}^{n,m,k-1}J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q^{(r-i)(k-1-i)}\binom{k-r}{k-1-i}_{q}\binom{r}{i}_{q}J_{r}^{n,m,k}.

In this equation, the product of the
q
-binomial coefficients vanishes
whenever
r>i+1
or
r<i.
Therefore, the above summation contains
only two nonzero terms, namely,

J_{i}^{n,m,k-1}J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}=\sum_{r\in\{i,i+1\}}q^{(r-i)(k-1-i)}\binom{k-r}{k-1-i}_{q}\binom{r}{i}_{q}J_{r}^{n,m,k}.

Simplifying,

J_{i}^{n,m,k-1}J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}=\frac{q^{k-i}-1}{q-1}J_{i}^{n,m,k}+q^{k-1-i}\cdot\frac{q^{i+1}-1}{q-1}J_{i+1}^{n,m,k}.

Applying this matrix equation to a vector
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}

gives

0=\frac{q^{k-i}-1}{q-1}J_{i}^{n,m,k}x+q^{k-1-i}\cdot\frac{q^{i+1}-1}{q-1}J_{i+1}^{n,m,k}x,

which directly implies (
5.25
).
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
n\geqslant m\geqslant k
be positive integers.
Then for all
r=0,1,\ldots,k
and
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k},

(5.26)

J_{r}^{n,m,k}x=(-1)^{k-r}q^{\binom{k-r}{2}}\binom{k}{r}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
k-r
for fixed integers
n,m,k.
For
the base case
r=k,
the equality in (
5.26
)
is trivial. For the inductive step with
k-r>0,
we have

\displaystyle J_{r}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=-q^{k-r-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r+1}-1}{q^{k-r}-1}J_{r+1}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=-q^{k-r-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r+1}-1}{q^{k-r}-1}\cdot(-1)^{k-r-1}q^{\binom{k-r-1}{2}}\binom{k}{r+1}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-r}q^{\binom{k-r}{2}}\binom{k}{r}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x,

where the first step uses Lemma
5.12
, and
the second step applies the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
be arbitrary subspaces of dimension

m
and
\ell,
respectively. Recall from Fact
2.4

that for fixed
n,m,\ell
, the dimension of
A\cap B
is uniquely
determined by the dimension of
A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp}
. This makes
the subspace matrix
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
identical, up to a permutation
of the rows and columns, to the subspace matrix
J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}

for an appropriate function
\varphi^{\prime}
. We record this fact as our next
lemma. Its role in our work will be to simplify the calculation of
the singular values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m.\ell}
and the eigenvalues
of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
by reducing the general case to the case
m+\ell\leqslant n

and
m\leqslant n/2
, respectively.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers
with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be
given. Then:

(i)

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}=PJ_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}Q
,
where
P,Q
are permutation matrices and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

is defined by
\varphi^{\prime}(t)=\varphi(t+m+\ell-n);

(ii)

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}=PJ_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-m}P^{-1},

where
P
is a permutation matrix and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

is defined by
\varphi^{\prime\prime}(t)=\varphi(t+2m-n).

Proof.

Recall that for any
d\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\},
the map
S\mapsto S^{\perp}

is a bijection between the subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
of dimension

d
and those of dimension
n-d.
For subspaces
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}

of dimension
m
and
\ell,
respectively, we have

\displaystyle(J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\dim(A\cap B))

\displaystyle=\varphi(\dim(A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp})+m+\ell-n)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{\prime}(\dim(A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp}))

\displaystyle=(J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-\ell})_{A^{\perp},B^{\perp}},

where the second step uses Fact
2.4
.
Rewriting this conclusion in matrix form,

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}=[(J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-\ell})_{A^{\perp},B^{\perp}}]_{A,B},

where
A,B
range over all subspaces of dimension
m
and
\ell,

respectively. The matrix on the right-hand side is clearly
J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-\ell},

up to a reordering of the rows and columns. This settles
(i)
.

An argument analogous to the above yields

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}=[(J_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-m})_{A^{\perp},B^{\perp}}]_{A,B},

where
A,B
range over all subspaces of dimension
m.
The matrix
on the right-hand side is the result of permuting the rows and columns
of
J_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}^{n,n-m,n-m}
according to the same permutation, which
is another way of phrasing
(ii)
.
∎

5.4.
Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of subspace matrices

Our description of the spectrum of each
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
is
in terms of a function which we now define.

Definition 5.15
.

For nonnegative integers
n,m,\ell,r,k
with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n

and
k\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\},
define

\Lambda_{r}^{n,m,\ell}(k)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}+(m-r)(\ell-r-i)}\binom{n-m-i}{\ell-r-i}_{q}\binom{m-k+i}{r-k+i}_{q}.

More generally, for any
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},
define

\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(r)\Lambda_{r}^{n,m,\ell}(k).

As part of our analysis of the eigenvalues of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m},

we will determine its eigenspaces and show that they are pairwise
orthogonal. The orthogonality will follow from the pairwise distinctness
of the corresponding eigenvalues, with the following lemma playing
a crucial role.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
n,m
be nonnegative integers with

m\leqslant n/2
. Then the numbers
\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(k)
for
k=0,1,\ldots,m

are pairwise distinct.

Proof.

For
r=0
, the
q
-binomial coefficient
\binom{m-k+i}{r-k+i}_{q}

in Definition
5.15
vanishes unless
i=k
. As a result,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(k)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}q^{\binom{k}{2}+m(m-k)}\binom{n-m-k}{m-k}_{q}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}q^{\binom{k}{2}+m(m-k)}\binom{n-m-k}{n-2m}_{q}.

For
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\},
the
q
-binomial coefficient in the
last expression is clearly positive and a nonincreasing function of

k
, whereas the exponent of
q
is a strictly decreasing function
of
k.
It follows that the numbers
|\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(k)|
for

k=0,1,\ldots,m
form a strictly decreasing sequence.
∎

As in
[
12
]
, we treat the all-ones eigenvector separately.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
n,m,\ell,r
be nonnegative integers
with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n
. Then

(5.27)

\displaystyle J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\,\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=q^{(m-r)(\ell-r)}\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}\binom{m}{r}_{q}\mathbf{1}

(5.28)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{r}^{n,m,\ell}(0)\,\mathbf{1},

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=q^{(m-r)(\ell-r)}\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}\binom{m}{r}_{q}\binom{n}{m}_{q}.

More generally, for
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},

(5.30)

\displaystyle J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}\,\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(0)\,\mathbf{1},

(5.31)

\displaystyle\|J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}|\varphi(r)|\,q^{(m-r)(\ell-r)}\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}\binom{m}{r}_{q}\binom{n}{m}_{q}.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
be a subspace of dimension
m.

By definition,
(J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\,\mathbf{1})_{A}
is the number of
\ell
-dimensional
subspaces
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
with
\dim(A\cap X)=r.

Taking
S^{\prime}=A
and
S=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
in Corollary
5.8
,
we obtain

(J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\,\mathbf{1})_{A}=q^{(m-r)(\ell-r)}\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}\binom{m}{r}_{q}.

This settles (
5.27
), which in turn implies (
5.28
)
by Definition
5.15
. Since there are exactly
\binom{n}{m}_{q}

subspaces
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
of dimension
m,
equation (
5.29
)
is immediate from (
5.27
). Equation (
5.30
)
follows by linearity from (
5.19
) and (
5.28
).
Analogously, (
5.31
) follows from (
5.19
)
and (
5.29
) since the matrices
J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}

for
r\geqslant 0
have disjoint support.
∎

The following lemma is the cornerstone of our analysis of the spectrum
of subspace matrices
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
. It generalizes Knuth’s
work from sets to subspaces (
m=\ell
) and further to the asymmetric
case of interest to us (
m\neq\ell
).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be positive integers with

n\geqslant m+\ell.
Let
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}\}
and
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}

be given. Then for all integers
t\geqslant 0,

(5.32)

J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x=\Lambda_{t}^{n,m,\ell}(k)J_{k}^{n,m,k}x.

More generally, for all
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},

(5.33)

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)J_{k}^{n,m,k}x.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary integer
t\geqslant 0
and define
M=J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}.

Let us compute the generic entry
M_{A,B}
, where
A,B
are subspaces
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
of dimension
m
and
k
, respectively.
By definition,
M_{A,B}
is the number of
\ell
-dimensional subspaces

X
such that
\dim(A\cap X)=t
and
B\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}.

Lemma
5.6
implies that

(J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k})_{A,B}=q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-k+r)}\binom{n-m-k+r}{\ell-t-k+r}_{q}\binom{m-r}{t-r}_{q},

where
r=\dim(A\cap B).
Rewriting this equation in matrix form,
we obtain

J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-k+r)}\binom{n-m-k+r}{\ell-t-k+r}_{q}\binom{m-r}{t-r}_{q}J_{r}^{n,m,k}.

Applying this matrix identity to a vector
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}
,
we find

\displaystyle J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-k+r)}\binom{n-m-k+r}{\ell-t-k+r}_{q}\binom{m-r}{t-r}_{q}J_{r}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-k+r)}\binom{n-m-k+r}{\ell-t-k+r}_{q}\binom{m-r}{t-r}_{q}\cdot(-1)^{k-r}q^{\binom{k-r}{2}}\binom{k}{r}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-k+r)}\binom{n-m-k+r}{\ell-t-k+r}_{q}\binom{m-r}{t-r}_{q}\cdot(-1)^{k-r}q^{\binom{k-r}{2}}\binom{k}{k-r}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}q^{(m-t)(\ell-t-i)}\binom{n-m-i}{\ell-t-i}_{q}\binom{m-k+i}{t-k+i}_{q}\cdot(-1)^{i}q^{\binom{i}{2}}\binom{k}{i}_{q}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{t}^{n,m,\ell}(k)\,J_{k}^{n,m,k}x,

where the second step uses Corollary
5.13
,
the fourth step is a change of variable, and the last step is immediate
by Definition
5.15
. This settles (
5.32
).
Now for any
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
,

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x=\sum_{t=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(t)J_{t}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x=\sum_{t=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(t)\Lambda_{t}^{n,m,\ell}(k)J_{k}^{n,m,k}x=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)J_{k}^{n,m,k}x,

where the first step uses (
5.19
), the second step
applies (
5.32
), and the last step is valid by
Definition
5.15
.
∎

We are now in a position to describe the eigenvalues of every symmetric
subspace matrix.

Theorem 5.19

(Eigenvalues of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
)
.

Let
n\geqslant m\geqslant 0
be given integers.

(i)

If
m\leqslant n/2,
then the eigenvalues of

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
for a given function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

are
\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}(k)
for
k=0,1,\ldots,m,
with corresponding
multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}
for
k=0,1,\ldots,m.

(ii)

If
m\geqslant n/2,
then the eigenvalues of

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
for a given function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

are
\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-m}(k)
for
k=0,1,\ldots,n-m,
with
corresponding multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,n-m,
with
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
given
by
\psi(t)=\varphi(t+2m-n).

Proof.

We first show that
(i)
implies
(ii)
.
Recall from Lemma
5.14
that
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}

is permutation-similar to
J_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-m}
with
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

given by
\psi(t)=\varphi(t+2m-n).
The eigenvalues of
J_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-m}

are, by part
(i)
of this theorem,
\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-m}(k)

for
k=0,1,\ldots,n-m,
with corresponding multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,n-m
. It follows that these are also the eigenvalues
of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
because a similarity transformation preserves
the eigenvalues and their multiplicities. This settles
(ii)
.

It remains to prove
(i)
, where by hypothesis

(5.34)

m\leqslant\frac{n}{2}.

Define subspaces
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
of the
\binom{n}{m}_{q}
-dimensional
real vector space by

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=\Span\{\mathbf{1}\},

\displaystyle S_{k}

\displaystyle=\{J_{k}^{n,m,k}x:x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}\},

\displaystyle k=1,2,\ldots,m.

Claim 5.20
.

Let
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
. Then
\dim S_{k}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}.

Proof.

We need only consider
k\geqslant 1,
the claim being trivial otherwise.
Observe from (
5.34
) and Lemma
5.11
that

J_{k}^{n,m,k}
has rank
\binom{n}{k}_{q}.
Put another way, its
columns are linearly independent. Since
S_{k}
is the image of
\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}

under
J_{k}^{n,m,k},
it follows that

(5.35)

\dim S_{k}=\dim\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}.

Another appeal to (
5.34
) and Lemma
5.11

reveals that the columns of
J_{k-1}^{n,k,k-1}
are linearly independent.
This makes
J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}=(J_{k-1}^{n,k,k-1})^{{}^{\sf T}}
a matrix
of order
\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}\times\binom{n}{k}_{q}
with linearly
independent rows, whence
\dim\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}
.
In view of (
5.35
), the proof is complete.
∎

Claim 5.21
.

Let
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
. Then every vector
of
S_{k}
is an eigenvector of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
with eigenvalue

\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}(k).

Proof.

For
k=0
, the claim is immediate from (
5.30
)
of Proposition
5.17
. Consider now the complementary
case
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}.
Here,
n
and
m
are positive integers.
Invoking Lemma
5.18
with
\ell=m
and (
5.34
)
yields
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}v=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}(k)v
for all
v\in S_{k},

as desired.
∎

Claim 5.22
.

For any
k,k^{\prime}\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
with
k\neq k^{\prime},

the subspaces
S_{k}
and
S_{k^{\prime}}
are orthogonal.

Proof.

Taking
\varphi=\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}
in Claim
5.21
shows that

S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
are eigenspaces of the symmetric matrix

J_{0}^{n,m,m}
with eigenvalues
\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(0),\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(1),\ldots,\Lambda_{0}^{n,m,m}(m),

respectively. But these
m+1
numbers are pairwise distinct by (
5.34
)
and Lemma
5.16
. It now follows from Fact
2.5

that
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
are pairwise orthogonal.
∎

As we just established with Claim
5.22
, the subspaces

S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
are pairwise orthogonal. Since they are
subspaces over the
reals,
we infer that
\dim(\sum_{k=0}^{m}S_{k})=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\dim S_{k}.

Using
\dim S_{k}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}
from Claim
5.20
,
we arrive at

\dim\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m}S_{k}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\left(\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}\right)=\binom{n}{m}_{q}-\binom{n}{-1}_{q}=\binom{n}{m}_{q}.

In other words, a basis for the vector space in question can be obtained
by concatenating bases for
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}.
Lastly, recall
from Claim
5.21
that
S_{k}
(for
k=0,1,\ldots,m
)
is an eigenspace of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}
with eigenvalue
\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,m}(k).

This settles
(i)
and completes the proof of
the theorem.
∎

At last, we adapt the previous proof to the asymmetric case (
m\neq\ell
)
and determine the singular values of every subspace matrix
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
.

Theorem 5.23

(Singular values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
)
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers
with
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n
.

(i)

If
m+\ell\leqslant n,
then the singular
values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
for a given function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

are

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)\,\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)},

\displaystyle k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\},

with corresponding multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

(ii)

If
m+\ell\geqslant n,
then the singular
values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
for a given function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

are

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}(k)\,\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-\ell,n-m}(k)},

\displaystyle k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{n-m,n-\ell\},

with corresponding multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{n-m,n-\ell\},
where
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

is given by
\psi(t)=\varphi(t+m+\ell-n).

Proof.

We first show that
(i)
implies
(ii)
.
Recall from Lemma
5.14
that the matrix

J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
is the same, up to a reordering of the rows
and columns, as
J_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}
with
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

given by
\psi(t)=\varphi(t+m+\ell-n).
The singular values of
J_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}

are, by part
(i)
of this theorem,
\sqrt{\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-m,n-\ell}(k)\,\Lambda_{\psi}^{n,n-\ell,n-m}(k)}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{n-m,n-\ell\},
with corresponding multiplicities

\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}
for
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{n-m,n-\ell\}
.
It follows that these are also the singular values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}

because reordering the columns or rows does not change the singular
values or their multiplicities. This establishes
(ii)
.

It remains to settle
(i)
, where by hypothesis

(5.36)

m+\ell\leqslant n.

We may further assume that

(5.37)

m\leqslant\ell,

for otherwise we can work with the transposed matrix
(J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})^{{}^{\sf T}}=J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}
,
the singular values being invariant under matrix transposition. By (
5.36
),
(
5.37
), and Fact
2.12
,

(5.38)

\binom{n}{m}_{q}\leqslant\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}.

Another consequence of (
5.36
) and (
5.37
)
is that
m\leqslant n/2,
which makes it possible to define subspaces

S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
of the
\binom{n}{m}_{q}
-dimensional
real vector space as in the proof of part
(i)

of Theorem
5.19
. In particular, Claims
5.20
–
5.22

apply as before.

Claim 5.24
.

Let
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
. Then every vector
of
S_{k}
is an eigenvector of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}

with eigenvalue
\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k).

Proof.

For
k=0
, a double application of Proposition
5.17

yields
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}\,\mathbf{1}=J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(0)\,\mathbf{1}=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(0)\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(0)\,\mathbf{1},

as desired. Consider now
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}.
In this case, due
to (
5.37
), the integers
n,m,\ell
are positive and
satisfy
\min\{m,\ell\}=m.
Then for any
x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k},

\displaystyle(J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m})J_{k}^{n,m,k}x

\displaystyle=J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}J_{k}^{n,m,k}x)

\displaystyle=J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{k}^{n,\ell,k}x

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)J_{k}^{n,m,k}x,

where the second and fourth steps apply Lemma
5.18

with (
5.36
) (note that the roles of
m
and
\ell

are reversed in the first application). We have shown that for each

x\in\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k},
its image under
J_{k}^{n,m,k}
is
an eigenvector of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}
with eigenvalue

\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k).
Since

S_{k}
is by definition the image of
\ker J_{k-1}^{n,k-1,k}
under

J_{k}^{n,m,k},
the claim is proved.
∎

Recall from Claims
5.20
and
5.22

that the subspaces
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
are pairwise orthogonal,
with
\dim S_{k}=\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}
. As in the
proof of Theorem
5.19
, this implies that
the real vector space in question is a direct sum of
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}.

In view of Claim
5.24
, we conclude that the eigenvalues
of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}
are
\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(k)\Lambda_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m}(k)

for
k=0,1,\ldots,m,
with corresponding multiplicities
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,m.
This completes the proof since the singular
values of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
are, by (
5.38
)
and Fact
2.6
, the square roots
of the eigenvalues of
J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}(J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})^{{}^{\sf T}}=J_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}J_{\varphi}^{n,\ell,m},

counting multiplicities.
∎

5.5.
Normalized subspace matrices

To study the communication complexity of the subspace intersection
problem, we now define normalized versions of subspace matrices.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers with
m+\ell\leqslant n,

and let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field. Define

(5.39)

\displaystyle\overline{J}_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\cdot J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell},

\displaystyle r=0,1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

For any function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},
define

(5.40)

\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(r)\overline{J}_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}.

The requirement
m+\ell\leqslant n
in Definition
5.25

serves to ensure that
J_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}\neq 0
for each

r=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\},
so that the normalization in (
5.39
)
is meaningful. As elsewhere in this manuscript, we will work with
the generic field
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{q}
and will henceforth
write
\overline{J}_{r}^{n,m,\ell}
and
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}

instead of
\overline{J}_{r}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
and
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
.

The following lemma relates the metric properties of a normalized
subspace matrix
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
to the corresponding
univariate function
\varphi
.

Lemma 5.26

(Metric properties of
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
)
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers
with
m+\ell\leqslant n.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be a given
function. Then:

(5.41)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n},m),\\
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n},\ell):\\
\dim(S\cap T)=r\end{subarray}}(\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}

\displaystyle=\varphi(r),

\displaystyle r=0,1,\dots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

Moreover,

(5.42)

\|\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}|\varphi(r)|.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{S,T:\dim(S\cap T)=r}(\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S,T:\dim(S\cap T)=r}\;\sum_{i=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\frac{\varphi(i)}{\|J_{i}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\cdot(J_{i}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S,T:\dim(S\cap T)=r}\frac{\varphi(r)}{\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\cdot(J_{r}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi(r)}{\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\sum_{S,T:\dim(S\cap T)=r}(J_{r}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}

(5.43)

\displaystyle=\varphi(r),

where the second step uses
(J_{i}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}=0
for
i\neq r,

and the final step is valid because
(J_{r}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}
equals

1
if
\dim(S\cap T)=r
and
0
otherwise. This proves (
5.41
).
An analogous argument yields

\sum_{S,T:\dim(S\cap T)=r}|(\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell})_{S,T}|=|\varphi(r)|.

Summing this equation over
r
gives (
5.42
).
∎

To describe the singular values of a normalized subspace matrix
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
,
we introduce a normalized counterpart of the function
\Lambda

from Definition
5.15
.

Definition 5.27
.

Let
n,m,\ell,k
be nonnegative integers with

m+\ell\leqslant n
and
k\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
. Define

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\Lambda_{r}^{n,m,\ell}(k),

\displaystyle r=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

More generally, for any
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R},
define

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{\varphi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{m,\ell\}}\varphi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k).

With this notation, we obtain the following counterpart
of Theorem
5.23
for normalized subspace matrices.

Theorem 5.28
.

Let
n,m,\ell
be nonnegative integers
with
m+\ell\leqslant n
. Let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be given.
Then the singular values of
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}
are:

\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\varphi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\,\overline{\Lambda}_{\varphi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)}

with multiplicity
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q},
where
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

Proof.

By definition,
\overline{J}_{\varphi}^{n,m,\ell}=J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,m,\ell}

with
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\varphi^{\prime}(r)=\begin{cases}\varphi(r)/\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}&\text{if }r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}\},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Recall from Theorem
5.23
that the singular values
of
J_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,m,\ell}
are
\sqrt{\Lambda_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,m,\ell}(k)\,\Lambda_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,\ell,m}(k)}

with multiplicity
\binom{n}{k}_{q}-\binom{n}{k-1}_{q},
where
k=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}.

Since
\Lambda_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,m,\ell}(k)=\overline{\Lambda}_{\varphi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

and
\Lambda_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{n,\ell,m}(k)=\overline{\Lambda}_{\varphi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)

by Definition
5.27
, the proof is complete.
∎

With our next lemma, we establish key algebraic and analytic properties
of
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)
.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
n,m,\ell,k
be nonnegative integers
with
m+\ell\leqslant n
and
k\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}.
Then
:

(i)

for
n,m,\ell,k
fixed,
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

as a function of
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}\}
is a polynomial
in
q^{r}
of degree at most
k;

(ii)

|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)|\leqslant 8\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}q^{-k(m-r)/2}

for
r=0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}
.

Proof.

Let
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\min\{m,\ell\}\}
be given. Then

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\Lambda_{r}^{n,m,\ell}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|J_{r}^{n,m,\ell}\|_{1}}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}+(m-r)(\ell-r-i)}\binom{n-m-i}{\ell-r-i}_{q}\binom{m-k+i}{r-k+i}_{q}

(5.44)

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}_{q}\frac{q^{\binom{i}{2}+(m-r)(\ell-r-i)}}{q^{(m-r)(\ell-r)}}\cdot\frac{\binom{n-m-i}{\ell-r-i}_{q}}{\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}}\cdot\frac{\binom{m-k+i}{r-k+i}_{q}}{\binom{m}{r}_{q}},

where the first step restates Definition
5.27
,
the second step applies Definition
5.15
, and the
final step uses Proposition
5.17
. To simplify (
5.44
),
observe that

(5.45)

\frac{\binom{n-m-i}{\ell-r-i}_{q}}{\binom{n-m}{\ell-r}_{q}}=\frac{q^{\ell-r}-1}{q^{n-m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q}{q^{n-m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q^{i-1}}{q^{n-m}-q^{i-1}}.

Indeed, if
\ell-r-i<0,
then the left-hand side is zero by definition,
and the right-hand side also evaluates to zero. In the complementary
case
\ell-r-i\geqslant 0,
one obtains (
5.45
)
directly from the definition of Gaussian binomial coefficients. One
analogously verifies that

(5.46)

\frac{\binom{m-k+i}{r-k+i}_{q}}{\binom{m}{r}_{q}}=\frac{q^{r}-1}{q^{m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q}{q^{m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q^{k-i-1}}{q^{m}-q^{k-i-1}},

by considering the cases
r-k+i<0
and
r-k+i\geqslant 0.
Substituting (
5.45
)
and (
5.46
) into (
5.44
)
gives

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}-(m-r)i}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-1}{q^{n-m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q}{q^{n-m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q^{i-1}}{q^{n-m}-q^{i-1}}

(5.47)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\frac{q^{r}-1}{q^{m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q}{q^{m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q^{k-i-1}}{q^{m}-q^{k-i-1}}.

To verify the algebraic property
(i)
,
rewrite (
5.47
) to obtain

\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)=\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}-mi}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell}-q^{r}}{q^{n-m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell}-q^{r+1}}{q^{n-m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{\ell}-q^{r+i-1}}{q^{n-m}-q^{i-1}}\\
\times\frac{q^{r}-1}{q^{m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q}{q^{m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q^{k-i-1}}{q^{m}-q^{k-i-1}}.\qquad

The
i
-th summand in this expression is, for fixed values of
n,m,\ell,k,

clearly a polynomial in
q^{r}
of degree at most
i+(k-i)=k.
This
settles
(i)
.

We now turn to the analytic property,
(ii)
.
Dropping the zero terms from the summation in (
5.47
),
and applying the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=\max\{0,k-r\}}^{\min\{k,\ell-r\}}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}-(m-r)i}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-1}{q^{n-m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q}{q^{n-m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{\ell-r}-q^{i-1}}{q^{n-m}-q^{i-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\frac{q^{r}-1}{q^{m}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q}{q^{m}-q}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{q^{r}-q^{k-i-1}}{q^{m}-q^{k-i-1}}.

The first
i
fractions on the right-hand side are each bounded by

q^{\ell-r}/q^{n-m},
whereas the other
k-i
fractions are each
bounded by
q^{r}/q^{m}
. Using these estimates leads to

\displaystyle|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=\max\{0,k-r\}}^{\min\{k,\ell-r\}}\binom{k}{i}_{q}q^{\binom{i}{2}-(m-r)i}\cdot q^{(\ell-r-n+m)i}\cdot q^{-(m-r)(k-i)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=\max\{0,k-r\}}^{\min\{k,\ell-r\}}4q^{i(k-i)+\binom{i}{2}-(m-r)i+(\ell-r-n+m)i-(m-r)(k-i)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=\max\{0,k-r\}}^{\min\{k,\ell-r\}}4q^{i(k-i)+\binom{i}{2}-(m-r)i-ri-(m-r)(k-i)}

(5.48)

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=\max\{0,k-r\}}^{\min\{k,\ell-r\}}4q^{i(k-r-i)+\binom{i}{2}-k(m-r)},

where the second step uses Corollary
2.14
, and the third
step is valid since
n\geqslant m+\ell
by hypothesis. Let
A(i)
denote
the exponent of
q
in (
5.48
). Then

A(i)
is an integer-valued function of
i
that strictly decreases
on
[k-r,\infty)
. As a result, (
5.48
)
yields

\displaystyle|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}4q^{A(\max\{0,k-r\})-t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}q^{A(\max\{0,k-r\})},

where the second step uses a geometric series along with
q\geqslant 2.

Therefore, the proof of
(ii)
will be
complete once we show that

(5.49)

A(\max\{0,k-r\})\leqslant-\frac{k(m-r)}{2}.

There are two cases to consider. If
k\leqslant r,
then
A(\max\{0,k-r\})=A(0)=-k(m-r)\leqslant-k(m-r)/2,

where the last step uses the hypothesis that
r\leqslant m.
If
k>r,

then
A(\max\{0,k-r\})=A(k-r)=\binom{k-r}{2}-k(m-r)\leqslant k(k-r)/2-k(m-r)\leqslant-k(m-r)/2
,
where the last step uses the hypothesis that
k\leqslant m.
This settles (
5.49
)
and completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.6.
Approximate trace norm of the subspace problem

We have reached a pivotal point in our study of the subspace intersection
problem, where we analyze the approximate trace norm of its characteristic
matrix. As in our analysis of the rank problem (Section
3
),
we start by constructing a suitable univariate dual object.

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
\Delta,R,d_{1},d_{2}
be nonnegative
integers with
0<R\leqslant\Delta-d_{1}-d_{2}
. Then there is a function

\psi\colon\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i)

\psi(0)=-1;

(ii)

\psi(R)>0;

(iii)

\psi(r)=0
for
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\setminus(\{R+d_{1}+1,R+d_{1}+2,\ldots,\Delta-d_{2}\}\cup\{0,R\});

(iv)

\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\psi(r)\xi(q^{r})=0

for every polynomial
\xi
of degree at most
\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2};

(v)

\sum_{r\in\{0,\ldots,\Delta\}\setminus\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|\leqslant 32q^{-d_{1}-1}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
d_{1}+d_{2}\leqslant\Delta-R<\Delta.
As a result, we
may invoke Lemma
3.9
with parameters
n,k,\ell,m
set
to
\Delta,\Delta-R,d_{2,}d_{1}
, respectively, to obtain a function

\varphi\colon\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i
′
)

\varphi(\Delta)=1;

(ii
′
)

\varphi(\Delta-R)<0;

(iii
′
)

\varphi(r)=0
for
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\setminus(\{d_{2},d_{2}+1,\ldots,\Delta-R-d_{1}-1\}\cup\{\Delta-R,\Delta\});

(iv
′
)

\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\varphi(r)\xi(q^{-r})=0
for every polynomial
\xi

of degree at most
\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2};

(v
′
)

\sum_{r\in\{0,\ldots,\Delta\}\setminus\{\Delta-R,\Delta\}}|\varphi(r)|\leqslant 32q^{-d_{1}-1}
.

Define
\psi\colon\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\psi(r)=-\varphi(\Delta-r).

Then
(i)
,
(ii)
,

(iii)
, and
(v)

are immediate from
(i′)
,
(ii′)
,

(iii′)
, and
(v′)
, respectively.
The remaining item
(iv)
follows from
(iv′)

via

\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\psi(r)\xi(q^{r})=-\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\varphi(\Delta-r)\xi(q^{r-\Delta}\cdot q^{\Delta})=-\sum_{i=0}^{\Delta}\varphi(i)\xi(q^{-i}\cdot q^{\Delta})=0.\qed

With the univariate dual object
\psi
now constructed, we will use
the associated subspace matrix
\Phi=\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}

as a dual witness to prove our sought lower bound on the approximate
trace norm. The theorem below only treats a canonical case of the
subspace intersection problem. However, we will see shortly that this
result allows us to tackle all parameter settings.

Theorem 5.31
.

Let
n,m,\ell,R
be given integers
with
0<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
and
m+\ell\leqslant n
. Let
F
be the
characteristic matrix of
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R}^{\mathbb{F}_{q},n,m,\ell}
.
Then for all reals
\delta\geqslant 0
and all nonnegative integers
d_{1},d_{2}

with
d_{1}+d_{2}\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}-R,

(5.50)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\left(1-\delta-\frac{64}{q^{d_{1}+1}}\right)\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\min\{m,\ell\}-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m+\ell-2\min\{m,\ell\}+2d_{2})/4},

(5.51)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1-\delta}{8}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(m+\ell-2R)/4}.

Proof.

Structurally, the proof is similar to that of Theorem
3.14
.
Let
\Delta=\min\{m,\ell\}.
Then
0<R\leqslant\Delta-d_{1}-d_{2}
by
hypothesis. Let
\psi\colon\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function
constructed in Lemma
5.30
, and extend
\psi
to
all of
\mathbb{Z}
by defining
\psi(r)=0
for
r\notin\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\dom F}F_{S,T}

\displaystyle(\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell})_{S,T}-\delta\|\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}\|_{1}-\sum_{\overline{\dom F}}|(\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell})_{S,T}|

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\dim(S\cap T)=0}(\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell})_{S,T}+\sum_{\dim(S\cap T)=R}(\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell})_{S,T}-\delta\|\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\dim(S\cap T)\notin\{0,R\}}|(\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell})_{S,T}|

\displaystyle=-\psi(0)+\psi(R)-\delta\|\psi\|_{1}-\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|

\displaystyle=|\psi(0)|+|\psi(R)|-\delta\|\psi\|_{1}-\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|

\displaystyle=(1-\delta)\|\psi\|_{1}-2\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|

(5.52)

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(1-\delta-2\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|\right)\|\psi\|_{1},

where the second step uses Lemma
5.26
, the third
step is valid by Lemma
5.30
(i)
–
(ii)
,
and the fifth step is justified by Lemma
5.30
(i)
.

We now analyze the spectral norm of
\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}
.
Recall from Lemma
5.29
(i)

that for fixed
n,m,\ell
and fixed
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\},

the quantity
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)
as a function
of
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}
is a polynomial in
q^{r}
of degree
at most
k.
As a result,

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}\}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\,\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)}

\displaystyle=\max_{k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}\}}\;\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)\cdot\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\psi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)}

\displaystyle=\max_{k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}\}}\;\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)\cdot 0}

(5.53)

\displaystyle=0,

where the first step applies Definition
5.27
,
and the second step uses Lemma
5.30
(iv)
.
Next, for all
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}
,

\displaystyle|\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\,\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\psi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\right|\cdot\left|\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta}\psi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta-d_{2}}\psi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\right|\cdot\left|\sum_{r=0}^{\Delta-d_{2}}\psi(r)\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\psi\|_{1}\max_{r=0,1,\ldots,\Delta-d_{2}}|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)|\cdot\|\psi\|_{1}\max_{r=0,1,\ldots,\Delta-d_{2}}|\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\psi\|_{1}^{2}\cdot 8\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{-1}q^{-k(m-\Delta+d_{2})/2}\cdot 8\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{-1}q^{-k(\ell-\Delta+d_{2})/2},

where the first step applies Definition
5.27
,
the second step uses Lemma
5.30
(iii)
,
and the last step invokes Lemma
5.29
(ii)

to bound
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)
and then again
(with the roles of
m
and
\ell
interchanged) to bound
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\{\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1,\ldots,\Delta-1,\Delta\}}\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\,\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leqslant\max_{k\in\{\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1,\ldots,\Delta-1,\Delta\}}\;8\|\psi\|_{1}\left(\binom{n}{m}_{q}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}q^{k(m-\Delta+d_{2})/2}q^{k(\ell-\Delta+d_{2})/2}\right)^{-1/2}

(5.54)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=8\|\psi\|_{1}\left(\binom{n}{m}_{q}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}q^{(\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m+\ell-2\Delta+2d_{2})/2}\right)^{-1/2}.

As a result,

\displaystyle\|\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}\|

\displaystyle=\max_{k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\Delta\}}\sqrt{\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}(k)\,\overline{\Lambda}_{\psi}^{\,n,\ell,m}(k)}

(5.55)

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\|\psi\|_{1}\left(\binom{n}{m}_{q}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}q^{(\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m+\ell-2\Delta+2d_{2})/2}\right)^{-1/2},

where the first step appeals to Theorem
5.28
,
and the second step substitutes the upper bounds from (
5.53
)
and (
5.54
).

We are now in a position to complete the proof of the theorem. Proposition
2.9

with
\Phi=\overline{J}_{\psi}^{\,n,m,\ell}
implies, in view of (
5.52
)
and (
5.55
), that

\|F\|_{\Sigma,\delta}\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\left(1-\delta-2\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|\right)\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\Delta-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m+\ell-2\Delta+2d_{2})/4}.

Since
\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|\leqslant 32q^{-d_{1}-1}
by Lemma
5.30
(v)
,
this settles (
5.50
). In the special case

d_{1}=0
and
d_{2}=\Delta-R,
we have
\sum_{r\notin\{0,R\}}|\psi(r)|=0

from Lemma
5.30
(iii)
,
whence (
5.51
).
∎

5.7.
Communication
lower bounds

We will now prove an optimal lower bound on the communication complexity
of the subspace intersection problem. To simplify the exposition,
we will first consider the canonical case where Alice and Bob need
to determine whether the intersection of their subspaces has dimension

0
versus dimension
R
, corresponding to the approximate trace
norm result that we just obtained. The general lower bound for all
parameter settings will then follow using the reduction of Proposition
2.26
.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be
a finite field with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements. Let
n,m,\ell,R

be nonnegative integers with

(5.56)

\displaystyle 0<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\},

(5.57)

\displaystyle R<\max\{m,\ell\},

(5.58)

\displaystyle m+\ell\leqslant n.

Then

(5.59)

Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})\geqslant c(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)\log q

for all
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5},1],
where
c>0

is an absolute constant independent of
\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell,R,\gamma.

Proof.

Due to the symmetry between
m
and
\ell
in the statement of the
lemma, we may assume that

(5.60)

m\geqslant\ell,

corresponding to the mnemonic “
m
for more,
\ell
for less.”
The hypotheses (
5.58
) ensures that there is
a pair of subspaces in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)

whose intersection has dimension
0
; analogously, (
5.56
)
and (
5.58
) ensure that there is a pair of subspaces
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)
whose
intersection has dimension
R.
This makes
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

a nonconstant function, with the trivial lower bound

(5.61)

Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})\geqslant 1.

It suffices to prove that the characteristic matrix
F
of this communication
problem satisfies

(5.62)

\|F\|_{\Sigma,1-\gamma}\geqslant c^{\prime}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{c^{\prime}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)}

for some absolute constant
c^{\prime}>0.
Indeed, once this lower bound
is established, an appeal to Theorem
2.23

yields

\displaystyle Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})

\displaystyle\qquad\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{c^{\prime}q^{c^{\prime}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)}}{3}

(5.63)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)\log q-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{3}{c^{\prime}}.

Taking a weighted arithmetic average of (
5.61
)
and (
5.63
) settles (
5.59
).

In what follows, we prove (
5.62
). We
first examine the case
\gamma\leqslant q^{-\ell+R+23}.
Equation (
5.51
)
of Theorem
5.31
yields

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,1-\gamma}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\gamma}{8}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(m+\ell-2R)/4}

(5.64)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{24}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(m+\ell-2R)/20},

where the second step uses the lemma hypothesis that
\gamma\geqslant\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle m+\ell-2R

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(m-R+1)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(m-R+1)\cdot\frac{1}{24}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)

(5.65)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{48}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1),

where the first step uses (
5.56
), the second step
is valid by (
5.57
) and (
5.60
),
the third step is legitimate because
\gamma\leqslant q^{-\ell+R+23}

in the case under consideration, and the last step uses the lemma
hypothesis that
\gamma\leqslant 1.
Equations (
5.64
)
and (
5.65
) imply (
5.62
)
for
c^{\prime}=1/960.

We now examine the complementary case,
\gamma\geqslant q^{-\ell+R+23}.

This assumption on
\gamma
, along with the lemma hypothesis that

\gamma\leqslant 1,
implies that the integer
d_{1}=\lfloor\log_{q}(128/\gamma)\rfloor

is an element of
\{0,1,2,\ldots,\ell-R\}.
This in turn means that
the integer
d_{2}=\lceil(\ell-R-d_{1})/2\rceil
is also an element
of
\{0,1,2,\ldots,\ell-R\}.
We have
d_{1}+d_{2}=d_{1}+\lceil(\ell-R-d_{1})/2\rceil\leqslant d_{1}+(\ell-R-d_{1})=\ell-R=\min\{m,\ell\}-R,

where the last step uses (
5.60
). As a result, Theorem
5.31

is applicable with parameters
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, and equation (
5.50
)
yields

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\Sigma,1-\gamma}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\left(\gamma-\frac{64}{q^{d_{1}+1}}\right)\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\min\{m,\ell\}-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m+\ell-2\min\{m,\ell\}+2d_{2})/4}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\left(\gamma-\frac{64}{q^{d_{1}+1}}\right)\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\ell-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m-\ell+2d_{2})/4}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\gamma}{16}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\ell-R-d_{1}-d_{2}+1)(m-\ell+2d_{2})/4}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\gamma}{16}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\ell-R-d_{1})(m-R-d_{1})/8}

(5.66)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\ell-R-\lfloor\log_{q}(128/\gamma)\rfloor)(m-R-\lfloor\log_{q}(128/\gamma)\rfloor)/8-\log_{q}(1/\gamma)},

where the second step applies (
5.60
), the third
and fifth steps use the definition of
d_{1},
and the fourth step
uses the definition of
d_{2}
. Recall that
\gamma\geqslant q^{-\ell+R+23}

in the case under consideration, and
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5},1]

by the lemma hypothesis. We may therefore use Claim
5.33
,
stated and proved below, to simplify to the right-hand side of (
5.66
)
as follows:

\|F\|_{\Sigma,1-\gamma}\geqslant\frac{1}{16}\binom{n}{m}_{q}^{1/2}\binom{n}{\ell}_{q}^{1/2}q^{(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)/160}.

This establishes (
5.62
) with
c^{\prime}=1/160,

completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Claim 5.33
.

For any
\gamma
with
\max\{q^{-\ell+R+23},\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5}\}\leqslant\gamma\leqslant 1,

(5.67)

\frac{1}{8}\left(\ell-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor\right)\left(m-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor\right)-\log_{q}\frac{1}{\gamma}\\
\geqslant\frac{1}{160}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1).\qquad

Proof.

The proof is somewhat tedious but straightforward. To begin with,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\left(\ell-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor\right)

\displaystyle\left(m-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor\right)-\log_{q}\frac{1}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left\lceil\log_{q}\frac{q^{\ell-R}\gamma}{128}\right\rceil\left(m-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor\right)-\log_{q}\frac{1}{\gamma}

(5.68)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\left\lceil\log_{q}\frac{q^{\ell-R}\gamma}{128}\right\rceil\left(m-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor-\frac{1}{2}\log_{q}\frac{1}{\gamma}\right),

where the last step uses the fact that
\frac{1}{8}\lceil\log_{q}(q^{\ell-R}\gamma/128)\rceil\geqslant 2

due to the hypothesis
\gamma\geqslant q^{-\ell+R+23}.
We now bound from
below the factors in (
5.68
). We have

(5.69)
