\log_{q}\frac{q^{\ell-R}\gamma}{128}\geqslant\log_{q}\frac{q^{\ell-R}\gamma}{q^{7}}\geqslant\log_{q}\frac{\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil}{q^{8}}=\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil-8\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1),

where the second and fourth steps are valid because
q^{\ell-R}\gamma\geqslant q^{23}

by hypothesis. The other factor in (
5.68
)
can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle m-R-\left\lfloor\log_{q}\frac{128}{\gamma}\right\rfloor-\frac{1}{2}\log_{q}\frac{1}{\gamma}

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R-\log_{q}\frac{q^{7}}{\gamma^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R-\log_{q}\frac{q^{7}}{(\max\{q^{-\ell+R+23},\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5}\})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R-7-\frac{3}{2}\min\left\{\ell-R-23,\frac{1}{5}(m+\ell-2R)+2\right\}

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R-7-\frac{3}{2}\min\left\{m-R-23,\frac{2}{5}(m-R)+2\right\}

\displaystyle\geqslant m-R-7-\frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}(m-R-23)+\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{2}{5}(m-R)+2\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{10}(m-R)+\frac{5}{2}

(5.70)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{10}(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1),

where the first step applies the bound
128\leqslant q^{7}
and drops
the floor operator, the second step uses the hypothesis for
\gamma,

the fourth step is valid by (
5.60
), the fifth step
replaces the minimum by a weighted average, and the last step is legitimate
because
\gamma\leqslant 1
by hypothesis. Now (
5.67
)
follows from (
5.68
)–(
5.70
).
∎

We now extend the previous lemma to all possible parameter settings,
thus obtaining the desired communication lower bound for subspace
intersection.

Theorem 5.34
.

Let
c>0
be the absolute
constant from Lemma
5.32
.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements,
and let
n,m,\ell,r,R
be integers with
\max\{0,m+\ell-n\}\leqslant r<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}.

Then
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.
Furthermore, for all
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5},1],

Q_{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}^{*}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{r,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})\geqslant\begin{cases}1&\text{if }R=m=\ell,\\
c(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)\log q&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

The hypothesis
\max\{0,m+\ell-n\}\leqslant r<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
implies
that
m+\ell-n\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
, which is equivalent to
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.

Recall from Proposition
2.25
that
for each integer
d\in[\max\{0,m+\ell-n\},\min\{m,\ell\}],
there
are subspaces
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)
and
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)

with
\dim(S\cap T)=d.
This makes
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{r,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}

a nonconstant function, which means that its
\varepsilon
-error quantum
communication complexity for each
\varepsilon\in[0,1/2)
is at least

1
bit. This settles the claimed communication lower bounds in the
case
R=m=\ell.

In what follows, we focus on the complementary case when
R,m,\ell

are not all equal. In view of
R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\},
we infer that

R<\max\{m,\ell\}.
This new inequality, and the theorem hypotheses
that
m+\ell-n\leqslant r<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
and
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/5},1]
,
can be equivalently stated as

(5.71)

\displaystyle R-r<\max\{m-r,\ell-r\},

(5.72)

\displaystyle 0<R-r\leqslant\min\{m-r,\ell-r\},

(5.73)

\displaystyle(m-r)+(\ell-r)\leqslant n-r,

(5.74)

\displaystyle\gamma\in[{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}q^{-((m-r)+(\ell-r)-2(R-r))/5},1].

Now

\displaystyle Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{r,R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})

\displaystyle\geqslant Q_{(1-\gamma)/2}^{*}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{0,R-r}^{\mathbb{F},n-r,m-r,\ell-r})

(5.75)

\displaystyle\geqslant c(\log_{q}\lceil q^{m-R}\gamma\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil q^{\ell-R}\gamma\rceil+1)\log q,

where the first step uses Proposition
2.26
,
and the second step is valid by Lemma
5.32

whose application is in turn justified by (
5.71
)–(
5.74
).
∎

Theorem
5.34
settles the
quantum

communication lower bound of Theorem
1.10

for the
promise
subspace intersection problem, and hence also
the
randomized
communication lower bound for the
total

subspace intersection problem.

5.8.
Communication
upper bounds for small error

In this section and the next, we prove communication upper bounds
matching our lower bound for the subspace intersection problem. We
start with a technical lemma.

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
n,m,\ell,r,\Delta
be nonnegative integers
with
r\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
and
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.
Fix a finite
field
\mathbb{F},
and let
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)
and
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)

be given subspaces. Let
X\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-r+\Delta)\times n}

and
Y\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\times(m+\ell-r+\Delta)}

be uniformly random matrices. Then with probability at least
1-16|\mathbb{F}|^{-\Delta-1},

one has

(5.76)

\displaystyle\dim(X(S))=\dim(S),

(5.77)

\displaystyle\dim(X(T))=\dim(T),

(5.78)

\displaystyle\dim(Y((X(S))^{\perp}))=\dim((X(S))^{\perp}),

(5.79)

\displaystyle\dim(Y((X(T))^{\perp}))=\dim((X(T))^{\perp}).

Assuming
(
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
,
the subspaces
S^{\prime}=Y((X(S))^{\perp})
and
T^{\prime}=Y((X(T))^{\perp})

satisfy

(5.80)

\displaystyle\dim(S^{\prime})=\ell-r+\Delta,

(5.81)

\displaystyle\dim(T^{\prime})=m-r+\Delta,

\displaystyle\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(X(S+T))

(5.82)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})-\dim(Y((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})).

Proof.

Abbreviate
q=|\mathbb{F}|.
Let
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4}
be the
events that correspond to (
5.76
)–(
5.79
),
respectively. Applying Lemma
2.20
with

t=m-1
and
d=m+\ell-r+\Delta
gives

(5.83)

\displaystyle\Prob[\neg E_{1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4q^{-(\ell-r+\Delta+1)}\leqslant 4q^{-\Delta-1}.

Analogously, applying Lemma
2.20
with

t=\ell-1
and
d=m+\ell-r+\Delta
shows that

(5.84)

\displaystyle\Prob[\neg E_{2}]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4q^{-(m-r+\Delta+1)}\leqslant 4q^{-\Delta-1}.

Conditioned on
E_{1}\wedge E_{2},
we have

(5.85)

\displaystyle\dim((X(S))^{\perp})=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(X(S))=\ell-r+\Delta,

(5.86)

\displaystyle\dim((X(T))^{\perp})=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(X(T))=m-r+\Delta.

As a result, invoking Lemma
2.20
with

t=\ell-r+\Delta-1
and
d=m+\ell-2r+3\Delta
shows that

(5.87)

\Prob[\neg E_{3}\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]\leqslant 4q^{-(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)+(\ell-r+\Delta-1)}\leqslant 4q^{-2\Delta-1}.

Analogously, invoking Lemma
2.20
with

t=m-r+\Delta-1
and
d=m+\ell-2r+3\Delta
shows that

(5.88)

\Prob[\neg E_{4}\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]\leqslant 4q^{-(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)+(m-r+\Delta-1)}\leqslant 4q^{-2\Delta-1}.

Now

\displaystyle\Prob[E_{1}\wedge E_{2}\wedge E_{3}\wedge E_{4}]

\displaystyle=\Prob[E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]\Prob[E_{3}\wedge E_{4}\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]

\displaystyle\geqslant\Prob[E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]-\Prob[\neg(E_{3}\wedge E_{4})\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\Prob[\neg E_{1}]-\Prob[\neg E_{2}]-\Prob[\neg E_{3}\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]-\Prob[\neg E_{4}\mid E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-16q^{-\Delta-1},

where the last step uses (
5.83
)–(
5.88
).
This settles the first part of the lemma.

In what follows, we assume (
5.76
)–(
5.79
).
Then (
5.80
) follows from
\dim(S^{\prime})=\dim((X(S))^{\perp})=\ell-r+\Delta,

where the last step uses (
5.85
). Analogously,
(
5.81
) follows from
\dim(T^{\prime})=\dim((X(T))^{\perp})=m-r+\Delta,

where the last step uses (
5.86
). Toward the
remaining equation (
5.82
), we have

\displaystyle\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\dim(((X(S))\cap(X(T)))^{\perp})

\displaystyle=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim((X(S))\cap(X(T)))

\displaystyle=m+\ell-r+\Delta-(\dim(X(S))+\dim(X(T))-\dim(X(S)+X(T)))

\displaystyle=-r+\Delta+\dim(X(S+T)),

where the first step uses Fact
2.4
,
and the last step uses (
5.76
), (
5.77
),
and the linearity of
X.
With this substitution, (
5.82
)
is equivalent to

(5.89)

\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})=m+\ell-2r+2\Delta-\dim(Y((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})).

Due to (
5.80
), (
5.81
), and
Y((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})=Y((X(S))^{\perp})+Y((X(T))^{\perp})=S^{\prime}+T^{\prime}
,
equation (
5.89
) is a restatement
of
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})=\dim(S^{\prime})+\dim(T^{\prime})-\dim(S^{\prime}+T^{\prime}),
which is a well-known
identity valid for any subspaces
S^{\prime},T^{\prime}.

∎

We are now ready to prove our communication upper bound for subspace
intersection in the regime where the error probability is a small
constant or tends to
0
. In the next section, we will generalize
this result to the more challenging regime where the error tends to

1/2
.

Theorem 5.36

(Small error)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be
a finite field with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements. Let
n,m,\ell,R

be integers with
0<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
and
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n
.
Then for each
0<\varepsilon\leqslant 1/3,

(5.90)

R_{\varepsilon}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O\left(\left(m-R+\left\lceil\log_{q}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil\right)\left(\ell-R+\left\lceil\log_{q}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil\right)\log q\right).

If in addition
m=\ell=R,
then for each
0<\varepsilon\leqslant 1/3,

(5.91)

R_{\varepsilon}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).

Proof.

Define
r=R-1.
For an integer
\Delta\geqslant 0
to be set later, consider
the following protocol
\Pi.
On input a pair of subspaces
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)

for Alice and
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)
for Bob, the parties
use their shared randomness to pick independent and uniformly random
matrices
X\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-r+\Delta)\times n}
and
Y\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\times(m+\ell-r+\Delta)}
.
Next, they verify the four conditions (
5.76
)–(
5.79
).
This can be done using only two bits of communication, with Alice
and Bob verifying the conditions pertaining to their respective inputs.
If any of these conditions fail, they output a uniformly random value
in
\{-1,1\}.
In the complementary case, Alice and Bob compute

\displaystyle S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=Y((X(S))^{\perp}),

\displaystyle T^{\prime}

\displaystyle=Y((X(T))^{\perp}),

respectively. The owner of the smaller of the subspaces
S^{\prime}
and

T^{\prime}
sends it to the other party in the form of a basis, who then
computes
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})
and outputs
1
if and only if
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})\leqslant\Delta.

We first analyze the communication cost of
\Pi.
If any of the conditions (
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
fail, the communication cost is
2
bits. If all four conditions
hold, then
\dim(S^{\prime})=\ell-r+\Delta
and
\dim(T^{\prime})=m-r+\Delta
by
Lemma
5.35
. As a result, a basis for the smaller of
the subspaces
S^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
can be communicated using
(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)(\min\{m,\ell\}-r+\Delta)\lceil\log q\rceil

bits, where the first factor is the dimension of the ambient space.
Altogether, the communication cost is at most

(5.92)

2+(2\max\{m,\ell\}-2r+3\Delta)(\min\{m,\ell\}-r+\Delta)\lceil\log q\rceil+1\\
=O((m-r+\Delta)(\ell-r+\Delta)\log q).\qquad

We now analyze the correctness probability. To this end, we prove
the following claim.

Claim 5.37
.

The output of the protocol
is correct whenever the matrices
X,Y
satisfy (
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
as well as the additional conditions

(5.93)

\displaystyle\dim(X(S+T))\geqslant\min\{\dim(S+T),m+\ell-r\},

(5.94)

\displaystyle\dim(Y((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp}))=\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp}).

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
5.35
that (
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
force (
5.82
), which in view of (
5.94
)
simplifies to

(5.95)

\displaystyle\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(X(S+T)).

We first consider the case
\dim(S\cap T)\leqslant r.
Here
\dim(S+T)\geqslant m+\ell-r,

which along with (
5.93
) implies that
\dim(X(S+T))\geqslant m+\ell-r.

Substituting this lower bound into (
5.95
)
gives
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})\leqslant\Delta
. As a result,
\Pi
outputs the
correct value in this case.

In the complementary case
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant r+1,
we have
\dim(S+T)\leqslant m+\ell-r-1

and therefore also
\dim(X(S+T))\leqslant m+\ell-r-1.
Substituting this
upper bound into (
5.95
) gives
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})\geqslant\Delta+1,

showing that the output of
\Pi
is correct in this case as well.
∎

Condition (
5.93
) fails with probability at
most
4q^{-\Delta-1}
, by Lemma
2.20

with
d=m+\ell-r+\Delta
and
t=\min\{\dim(S+T),m+\ell-r\}-1
. Moreover,
conditioned on (
5.76
) and (
5.77
),
one has

\displaystyle\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})

\displaystyle\leqslant\dim((X(S))^{\perp})+\dim((X(T))^{\perp})

\displaystyle=2(m+\ell-r+\Delta)-\dim(X(S))-\dim(X(T))

\displaystyle\leqslant m+\ell-2r+2\Delta

and hence (
5.94
) fails with probability at
most
4q^{-(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)+(m+\ell-2r+2\Delta-1)}\leqslant 4q^{-\Delta-1}
,
by Lemma
2.20
with
d=m+\ell-2r+3\Delta

and
t=\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})-1
. Since (
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
are simultaneously true with probability at least
1-16q^{-\Delta-1}

(by Lemma
5.35
), we conclude that the six conditions
(
5.76
)–(
5.79
),
(
5.93
), (
5.94
) hold
simultaneously with probability at least
1-16q^{-\Delta-1}-4q^{-\Delta-1}-4q^{-\Delta-1}=1-24q^{-\Delta-1}.

Now Claim
5.37
implies
that the described protocol
\Pi
has error probability at most
24q^{-\Delta-1}.

Since we calculated
\Pi
’s cost to be (
5.92
),
we conclude that

R_{24/q^{\Delta+1}}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O((m-r+\Delta)(\ell-r+\Delta)\log q).\qquad

Taking
\Delta=\lfloor\log_{q}(24/\varepsilon)\rfloor
now settles (
5.90
).
For the additional upper bound (
5.91
),
observe that
\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell}
for
m=\ell=R

is the equality problem with domain
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m).

The claimed upper bound now follows because it is well-known
[
14
, Chapter 3.3]

that the equality problem over any domain has
\varepsilon
-error randomized
communication complexity
O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
.
∎

5.9.
Communication upper bounds for large error

To study the large-error regime, we recall a basic fact on vector
spaces.

Proposition 5.38
.

Let
A,A^{\prime}
be subspaces such that

A^{\prime}\subseteq A.
Then for any subspace
B,

(5.96)

\dim(A\cap B)-\dim(A^{\prime}\cap B)\leqslant\dim(A)-\dim(A^{\prime}).

Proof.

Since
A\cap B+A^{\prime}
is a subspace of
A,
we have
\dim(A\cap B+A^{\prime})\leqslant\dim(A).

Expanding the left-hand side yields
\dim(A\cap B)+\dim(A^{\prime})-\dim(A\cap B\cap A^{\prime})\leqslant\dim(A),

which is clearly equivalent to (
5.96
).
∎

We now revisit the subspaces
S^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
in Lemma
5.35

and study the distribution of
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.39
.

Let
n,m,\ell,r,\Delta
be nonnegative
integers with
r<\min\{m,\ell\}
and
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.
Fix
a finite field
\mathbb{F}
with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements, and
let
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)
and
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)

be given subspaces. Let
X\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-r+\Delta)\times n}

and
Y\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\times(m+\ell-r+\Delta)}

be uniformly random matrices. Let
Z
be the indicator random variable
for the event that
(
5.76
)–(
5.79
)

hold. Define
S^{\prime}=Y((X(S))^{\perp})
and
T^{\prime}=Y((X(T))^{\perp})
.
Then:

(i)

\Exp[Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}]\leqslant q^{\Delta}(1+8q^{-\Delta})^{2}

whenever
\dim(S\cap T)\leqslant r;

(ii)

\Exp[Zq^{\min\{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime}),\Delta+1\}}]\geqslant q^{\Delta+1}(1-16q^{-\Delta-1})

whenever
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant r+1.

Proof.

(i)
Consider the random variables

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=m+\ell-r-\min\{\dim(X(S+T)),m+\ell-r\},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})-\dim(Y((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})).

Then the inequality

(5.97)

Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}\leqslant Zq^{A+B+\Delta}

is trivially true for
Z=0
and follows from equation (
5.82
)
of Lemma
5.35
for
Z=1.
The hypothesis
\dim(S\cap T)\leqslant r

implies that
\dim(S+T)=\dim(S)+\dim(T)-\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant m+\ell-r.

As a result, applying Lemma
2.20
with

d=m+\ell-r+\Delta
and
T=m+\ell-r
gives

(5.98)

\Exp_{X}[q^{A}]\leqslant 1+8q^{-\Delta}.

Now, let
Z^{\prime}
be the indicator random variable for the event that (
5.76
)
and (
5.77
) hold. Then
Z^{\prime}=1
implies that

\displaystyle\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})

\displaystyle\leqslant\dim((X(S))^{\perp})+\dim((X(T))^{\perp})

\displaystyle=2(m+\ell-r+\Delta)-\dim(X(S))-\dim(X(T))

\displaystyle\leqslant m+\ell-2r+2\Delta.

As a result, Lemma
2.20
is applicable
with
d=m+\ell-2r+3\Delta
and
T=\dim((X(S))^{\perp}+(X(T))^{\perp})

and gives

(5.99)

Z^{\prime}\Exp_{Y}[q^{B}\mid X]\leqslant Z^{\prime}(1+8q^{-\Delta}).

It remains to put these ingredients together:

\displaystyle\Exp[Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Exp[Zq^{A+B+\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Exp[Z^{\prime}q^{A+B+\Delta}]

\displaystyle=q^{\Delta}\Exp_{X}q^{A}Z^{\prime}\Exp_{Y}[q^{B}\mid X]

\displaystyle\leqslant q^{\Delta}\Exp_{X}q^{A}Z^{\prime}(1+8q^{-\Delta})

\displaystyle\leqslant q^{\Delta}\Exp_{X}q^{A}(1+8q^{-\Delta})

\displaystyle\leqslant q^{\Delta}(1+8q^{-\Delta})^{2},

where the first step uses (
5.97
), the second step
is justified by
Z\leqslant Z^{\prime},
the fourth step applies (
5.99
),
and the last step uses (
5.98
).

(ii)
Assume now that
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant r+1
.
For
Z=1,
equation (
5.82
) of Lemma
5.35

gives

\displaystyle\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geqslant m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(X(S+T))

\displaystyle\geqslant m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(S+T)

\displaystyle=m+\ell-r+\Delta-\dim(S)-\dim(T)+\dim(S\cap T)

\displaystyle=-r+\Delta+\dim(S\cap T)

\displaystyle\geqslant\Delta+1.

Now

\displaystyle\Exp[Zq^{\min\{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime}),\Delta+1\}}]

\displaystyle\geqslant q^{\Delta+1}\Exp[Z]

\displaystyle\geqslant q^{\Delta+1}(1-16q^{-\Delta-1}),

where the second step uses Lemma
5.35
.
∎

At last, we are in a position to prove our claimed communication upper
bound for the subspace intersection problem.

Theorem 5.40

(Large error)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be
a finite field with
q=|\mathbb{F}|
elements, and let
n,m,\ell,R

be integers with
\max\{0,m+\ell-n\}<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}.
Then
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n

and

(5.100)

R_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{16q^{m+\ell-2R+16}}}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})\leqslant 2.

Furthermore, for each
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/3},\frac{1}{3}],

(5.101)

R_{(1-\gamma)/2}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O((\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{m-R}\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{\ell-R}\rceil+1)\log q).

If in addition
m=\ell=R,
then

(5.102)

R_{1/3}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O(1).

Proof.

The hypothesis
\max\{0,m+\ell-n\}<R\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
implies that

m+\ell-n\leqslant\min\{m,\ell\}
, which is equivalent to
\max\{m,\ell\}\leqslant n.

The bound (
5.102
) is immediate from Theorem
5.36
.

In the rest of the proof, define
r=R-1
. We will first settle (
5.100
).
Let
\Delta
be a nonnegative integer to be chosen later. Consider
the following protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
. On input a pair of subspaces
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},m)

for Alice and
T\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{F}^{n},\ell)
for Bob, the parties
use their shared randomness to pick independent and uniformly random
matrices
X\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-r+\Delta)\times n}
and
Y\in\mathbb{F}^{(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\times(m+\ell-r+\Delta)}
.
Alice and Bob compute
S^{\prime}=Y((X(S))^{\perp})
and
T^{\prime}=Y((X(T))^{\perp}),

respectively. Note that
S^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
are subspaces in an ambient
vector space
V
of dimension
m+\ell-2r+3\Delta.
Let
Z
be the
indicator random variable for the event that the four conditions (
5.76
)–(
5.79
)
hold. Alice and Bob use shared randomness to pick a uniformly random
vector
v\in V
. They output
1
in the event that
Z=1
and
v\in S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime},

and output a uniformly random
\pm 1
value otherwise. The communication
cost of this protocol is
2
bits since Alice can privately verify
the conditions (
5.76
), (
5.78
),
and
v\in S^{\prime},
and likewise Bob can privately verify the conditions
(
5.77
), (
5.79
), and
v\in T^{\prime}
.
Observe further that

\Exp[\Pi^{\prime}(S,T)\mid X,Y]=Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})-(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)}.

Passing to expectations over
X
and
Y,
we arrive at

\Exp\Pi^{\prime}(S,T)=q^{-(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)}\Exp[Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}].

Applying Lemma
5.39
, we find that
\Pi^{\prime}(S,T)

has expectation at most
\alpha^{\prime}=q^{-m-\ell+2r-2\Delta}(1+8q^{-\Delta})^{2}

if
\dim(S\cap T)\leqslant r,
and at least
\beta^{\prime}=q^{-m-\ell+2r-2\Delta}q(1-16q^{-\Delta-1})

if
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant r+1.
Taking
\Delta=7,
one calculates that

\beta^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant q^{-m-\ell+2r-14}/2
. Now Proposition
2.24

implies that

R_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{16q^{m+\ell-2r+14}}}(\neg\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})\leqslant 2,

which is equivalent to (
5.100
).

In what follows, we prove the remaining upper bound (
5.101
).
Due to the symmetry between
m
and
\ell
, we may assume without
loss of generality that

(5.103)

m\geqslant\ell.

Let
k
and
\Delta
be nonnegative integers to be set later, where

(5.104)

1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell-r.

We will adapt
\Pi^{\prime}
to obtain a new protocol
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
that satisfies
the following inequalities for all subspaces
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n}

of dimension
m
and
\ell,
respectively:

(5.105)

\displaystyle\Exp[\Pi^{\prime\prime}(S,T)\mid X,Y]\geqslant q^{-k-\Delta}Zq^{\min\{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime}),\Delta+1\}},

(5.106)

\displaystyle\Exp[\Pi^{\prime\prime}(S,T)\mid X,Y]\leqslant q^{-k-\Delta}Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}.

On input
S
and
T,
Alice and Bob in
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
choose uniformly
random matrices
X
and
Y
as before. They then compute the indicator
random variable
Z,
which is a function of
X,Y,S,T.
If
Z=0,

they output a uniformly random
\pm 1
value. Clearly, (
5.105
)
and (
5.106
) hold in this case.

In the complementary case
Z=1,
Alice and Bob compute
S^{\prime}=Y((X(S))^{\perp})

and
T^{\prime}=Y((X(T))^{\perp}),
respectively. Lemma
5.35

with
Z=1
implies that
S^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
are subspaces of dimension

\ell-r+\Delta
and
m-r+\Delta
, respectively, in an ambient vector
space of dimension
m+\ell-2r+3\Delta.
This makes it possible for
Bob to find a subspace
U
of dimension
m-r+\Delta+k
such that

T^{\prime}\subseteq U
, and send
U
to Alice. An application of Proposition
5.38

yields

(5.107)

\dim(S^{\prime}\cap U)-\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})\leqslant\dim(U)-\dim(T^{\prime})=k.

What Alice does next depends on the dimension of
S^{\prime}\cap U.

(i)

If
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap U)\geqslant k+\Delta+1,
then (
5.107
)
implies that
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})\geqslant\Delta+1.
Therefore, (
5.105
)
and (
5.106
) amount to the requirement that the
protocol’s output have expectation at least
q^{-k+1}
and at most

q^{-k-\Delta+\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}\in[q^{-k+1},\infty).
To meet this
requirement, Alice simply outputs a random
\pm 1
value with expectation

q^{-k+1}
.

(ii)

If
\dim(S^{\prime}\cap U)\leqslant k+\Delta,
Alice identifies a
(k+\Delta)
-dimensional
subspace
S^{\prime\prime}
with the property that
S^{\prime}\cap U\subseteq S^{\prime\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
,
which exists because
k+\Delta\leqslant\ell-r+\Delta
due to (
5.104
).
She then picks a uniformly random vector
v\in S^{\prime\prime}
and sends it
to Bob, who outputs
1
if
v\in T^{\prime}
and a uniformly random
\pm 1

value otherwise. In this case, Alice and Bob’s expected output is

\frac{q^{\dim(S^{\prime\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}}{q^{\dim(S^{\prime\prime})}}=\frac{q^{\dim(S^{\prime\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}}{q^{k+\Delta}}=\frac{q^{\dim(S^{\prime\prime}\cap U\cap T^{\prime})}}{q^{k+\Delta}}=\frac{q^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap U\cap T^{\prime})}}{q^{k+\Delta}}=\frac{q^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}}{q^{k+\Delta}},

where the second step uses
T^{\prime}\subseteq U,
the third step uses the
defining property
S^{\prime}\cap U\subseteq S^{\prime\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
of the set

S^{\prime\prime},
and the last step is valid due to
T^{\prime}\subseteq U.
This agrees
with (
5.105
) and (
5.106
), which
require that the protocol’s output have expectation between
q^{-k-\Delta}q^{\min\{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime}),\Delta+1\}}

and
q^{-k-\Delta}q^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}.

The proof of (
5.105
) and (
5.106
)
is now complete.

Since
U
has co-dimension
\ell-r+2\Delta-k
, it can be communicated
in the form of a basis for
U^{\perp}
using
(\ell-r+2\Delta-k)(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\lceil\log q\rceil

bits. The vector
v
takes
(m+\ell-2r+3\Delta)\lceil\log q\rceil

bits to send. In view of (
5.103
), we conclude that

(5.108)

\cost(\Pi^{\prime\prime})=O((\ell-r+2\Delta-k+1)(m-r+\Delta)\lceil\log q\rceil+1).

Lastly, we will show that
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
is a distinguisher for the subspace
intersection problem. For this, pass to expectations with respect
to
X
and
Y
in (
5.105
) and (
5.106
)
to obtain

(5.109)

\displaystyle\Exp\Pi^{\prime\prime}(S,T)\geqslant q^{-k-\Delta}\Exp[Zq^{\min\{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime}),\Delta+1\}}],

(5.110)

\displaystyle\Exp\Pi^{\prime\prime}(S,T)\leqslant q^{-k-\Delta}\Exp[Zq^{\dim(S^{\prime}\cap T^{\prime})}].

Now Lemma
5.39
implies that
\Pi^{\prime\prime}(S,T)

has expectation at most
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=q^{-k}(1+8q^{-\Delta})^{2}
if

\dim(S\cap T)\leqslant r,
and at least
\beta^{\prime\prime}=q^{-k+1}(1-16q^{-\Delta-1})

if
\dim(S\cap T)\geqslant r+1.
Taking
\Delta=7,
one calculates that

\beta^{\prime\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}\geqslant q^{-k}/2
. Now (
5.108
)
and Proposition
2.24
imply that

(5.111)

\displaystyle R_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{16q^{k}}}(\neg\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})

\displaystyle=O((\ell-r-k+1)(m-r+1)\log q)

for every positive integer
k\leqslant\ell-r.

Let
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/3},\frac{1}{3}]
be given.
For
\gamma\in[q^{-4},\frac{1}{3}],
one obtains (
5.101
)
from the bound
R_{1/3}(\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})=O((\ell-R+1)(m-R+1)\log q)

of Theorem
5.36
. For
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/3},q^{-4}],

setting
k=\min\{\lfloor\log_{q}(1/\gamma)\rfloor-3,\ell-r\}
in (
5.111
)
gives

\displaystyle R_{(1-\gamma)/2}(\neg\operatorname{INTERSECT}_{R}^{\mathbb{F},n,m,\ell})

\displaystyle=O((\ell-r-\min\{\lfloor\log_{q}(1/\gamma)\rfloor-3,\ell-r\}+1)(m-r+1)\log q)

\displaystyle=O((\max\{\lceil\log_{q}(\gamma q^{\ell-r})\rceil,0\}+4)(m-r+1)\log q)

\displaystyle=O((\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{\ell-r}\rceil+1)(m-r+1)\log q)

\displaystyle=O((\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{\ell-R}\rceil+1)(m-R+1)\log q)

\displaystyle=O((\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{\ell-R}\rceil+1)(\log_{q}\lceil\gamma q^{m-R}\rceil+1)\log q),

where the last step uses (
5.103
) and
\gamma\geqslant\frac{1}{3}q^{-(m+\ell-2R)/3}.

This completes the proof of (
5.101
).
∎

Theorem
5.40
settles the
randomized

communication upper bounds of Theorem
1.10

for the
total
subspace intersection problem, and hence also
the
quantum
communication upper bound for the
promise

subspace intersection problem.

Appendix A
Fourier spectrum of nonsingularity

Throughout this section, the underlying field is
\mathbb{F}_{q}

for an arbitrary prime power
q.
The root of unity
\omega
and
the notation
\omega^{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
are as defined
in Section
2.4
. The objective of this appendix is to
prove Lemma
3.5
. Our proof is shorter and simpler than
the approach of Sun and Wang
[
27
]
,
who proved Lemma
3.5
for fields of prime order. What
our proofs have in common is the following proposition
[
27
]
.

Proposition A.1

(Sun and Wang)
.

For any integers
n\geqslant 1
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\},

\Gamma_{n}(n,r)=\Exp_{X\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\omega^{X_{1,1}+\cdots+X_{r,r}}.

Proof
(due to Sun and Wang)
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be independent and uniformly random nonsingular matrices
of order
n
. Then by Proposition
2.19
(ii)
,
the product
XI_{r}Y
is a uniformly random matrix of rank
r
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(n,r)

\displaystyle=\Exp\omega^{\langle Z,XI_{r}Y\rangle}

\displaystyle=\Exp\omega^{\langle X^{\sf T}ZY^{\sf T},I_{r}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\Exp\omega^{\langle X,I_{r}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\Exp\omega^{X_{1,1}+X_{2,2}+\cdots+X_{r,r}},

where the second step uses Fact
2.2
(ii)
,
and the third step is legitimate because
X^{\sf T}ZY^{\sf T}
is a uniformly
random nonsingular matrix by Proposition
2.19
(i)
.
∎

We are now ready to establish our result of interest.

Lemma

(restatement of Lemma
3.5
)
.

For any integers
n\geqslant 1
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(n,r)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r}q^{\binom{r}{2}}}{(q^{n}-1)(q^{n}-q)\cdots(q^{n}-q^{r-1})}.

Proof.

Consider independent random matrices
X
and
L
of order
n
,
where
X
is a uniformly random nonsingular matrix and
L
is a
uniformly random nonsingular
lower-diagonal
matrix. By Proposition
2.19
(i)
,
the product
XL
is a uniformly random nonsingular matrix. Therefore,
Proposition
A.1
implies that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(n,r)

\displaystyle=\Exp\omega^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(XL)_{i,i}}.

We will say that
X
is
nice
if
X_{i,j}=0
for all
(i,j)

pairs such that
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
and
j>i.

Claim A.2
.

One has

\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(XL)_{i,i}}\mid X\right]=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r}(q-1)^{-r}&\text{if $X$ is nice,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

This claim, to be proved shortly, implies that

(A.1)

\Gamma_{n}(n,r)=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{(q-1)^{r}}\Prob[X\text{ is nice}].

The probability of the nonsingular matrix
X
being nice is straightforward
to calculate: there are
q-1
choices for the first row,
q(q-1)

choices for the second row,
q^{2}(q-1)
choices for the third row,
and so on up to row
r
, whence

\displaystyle\Prob_{X}[X\text{ is nice}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}q^{i-1}(q-1)}{(q^{n}-1)(q^{n}-q)\cdots(q^{n}-q^{r-1})}.

Making this substitution in (
A.1
) completes
the proof.
∎

Proof of Claim
A.2
.

Conditioned on
X,
the columns of
XL
are independent random
variables. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(XL)_{i,i}}\mid X\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{(XL)_{i,i}}\mid X\right]

(A.2)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{\sum_{j=i}^{n}X_{i,j}L_{j,i}}\mid X\right].

The entries of
L
are independent random variables, with the diagonal
entries distributed uniformly on
\mathbb{F}_{q}\setminus\{0\}
and
the subdiagonal entries distributed uniformly on
\mathbb{F}_{q}.

If
X
is not nice, then
X_{i,k}\neq 0
for some
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}

and
k>i,
which means that the corresponding summation
\sum_{j=i}^{n}X_{i,j}L_{j,i}

is a uniformly random field element. This forces (
A.2
)
to vanish, due to (
2.14
). When
X
is nice, on
the other hand, (
A.2
)
simplifies as follows:

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{\sum_{j=i}^{n}X_{i,j}L_{j,i}}\mid X\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Exp_{L}\left[\omega^{X_{i,i}L_{i,i}}\mid X\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Exp_{a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\setminus\{0\}}\omega^{a_{i}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\omega^{a}-1}{q-1}\right)^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{(q-1)^{r}},

where the second step is legitimate because
X_{i,i}
is nonzero
and
L_{i,i}
is a uniformly random nonzero field element, and the
last step uses (
2.14
).
∎

Appendix B
Multiparty lower bounds via symmetrization

The purpose of this appendix is to prove Proposition
1.11
,
which gives a generic method for transforming two-party communication
lower bounds for a class of problems into corresponding multiparty
lower bounds. Recall that we adopt the number-in-hand blackboard model
of multiparty communication, reviewed in the introduction. The notation

R_{\varepsilon}(F)
stands for the
\varepsilon
-error randomized communication
complexity of the two-party or multiparty problem
F.
The
cost

of a protocol
\Pi,
denoted
\cost(\Pi)
, is the total number of
bits written to the blackboard in the worst-case execution of
\Pi.

Proposition

(restatement of Proposition
1.11
)
.

Let
(X,+)
be a finite Abelian group, and let
f\colon X\to\{-1,1,*\}

be a given function. For
t\geqslant 2,
let
F_{t}\colon X^{t}\to\{-1,1,*\}

be the
t
-party communication problem given by
F_{t}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t})=f(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{t}).

Then for all
t\geqslant 2,

R_{1/6}(F_{t})\geqslant\frac{1}{12}tR_{1/3}(F_{2}).

Proof.

The proof uses the symmetrization technique of Phillips, Verbin, and
Zhang
[
20
]
. Let
\Pi
be a randomized protocol
for
F_{t}
with error probability
1/6
. We will use
\Pi
to
construct a protocol for the two-party problem
F_{2}
with error
probability
1/3
and communication cost at most
\cost(\Pi)\cdot 12/t
.

The protocol for
F_{2}
is as follows. On input
(a,b)\in X\times X,

Alice and Bob use their shared randomness to pick uniformly random
elements
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{t-1}\in X
and uniformly random integers

i,j
with
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n.
Let

\mathbf{x}=(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{t-1},-r_{1}-r_{2}-\cdots-r_{t-1})+(0,\ldots,0,a,0,\ldots,0,b,0,\ldots,0),

where the rightmost tuple has
a
in the
i
-th component,
b

in the
j
-th component, and zeroes everywhere else. Since the components
of
\mathbf{x}
sum to
a+b,
we have

(B.1)

F_{t}(\mathbf{x})=F_{2}(a,b).

Moreover,
\mathbf{x}
is a
uniformly random
tuple whose components
sum to
a+b
because the first
t-1
components are distributed
independently and uniformly at random on
X
, whereas the sum of
the components is
a+b
. In particular,
\mathbf{x}
and
(i,j)

are independent random variables.

By construction, Alice knows all the components of
\mathbf{x}
except
for the
j
-th, and Bob knows all the components except for the
i
-th.
This makes it possible for them to run
\Pi
on
\mathbf{x},
with
Alice simulating all the parties other than the
j
-th, and Bob simulating
all the parties other than the
i
-th. When
\Pi
requires the
i
-th
party to speak, Alice sends his message to Bob, and analogously for
the
j
-th party. For
k=1,2,\ldots,t,
consider the random variable

C(\mathbf{x},k)
defined as the total number of bits sent in
\Pi

by the
k
-th party on input
\mathbf{x}
. Then the number of bits
exchanged by Alice and Bob is
C(\mathbf{x},i)+C(\mathbf{x},j).

Using the independence of
\mathbf{x}
and
(i,j),
we can now bound
Alice and Bob’s expected communication cost on input
(a,b)
as follows:

(B.2)

\Exp[C(\mathbf{x},i)+C(\mathbf{x},j)]=\frac{2}{t}\Exp[C(\mathbf{x},1)+\cdots+C(\mathbf{x},t)]\leqslant\frac{2}{t}\cost(\Pi).

By (
B.1
), the described two-party protocol computes

F_{2}
with the same error probability that
\Pi
computes
F_{t}
,
namely,
1/6.
Furthermore, by (
B.2
), the
expected

communication cost of the two-party protocol on any given input is
at most
\cost(\Pi)\cdot 2/t.
By Markov’s inequality, the probability
of Alice and Bob exchanging at least
\cost(\Pi)\cdot 12/t
bits is
at most
1/6.
Therefore, one can obtain a protocol for
F_{2}

with error
1/6+1/6=1/3
by terminating the described protocol as
soon as
\lfloor\cost(\Pi)\cdot 12/t\rfloor
bits have been communicated.
∎
